\subsection*{Comparative Synthesis and Critical Discussion}
\textcolor{red}{The six one-dimensional benchmark configurations compare the Finite-Difference Time-Domain (FDTD) scheme and the Pseudo-Spectral Time-Domain (PSTD) formulation across selected spatial regularities and physical damping regimes. The results summarized in Table~\ref{tab:convergence_analysis} support the following conclusions for these test cases:}

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textcolor{red}{\textbf{Exact temporal integration vs. algebraic truncation error}: For the selected configurations whose initial conditions are either finite modal expansions or sufficiently well resolved by the retained cosine basis (such as the smooth standing waves in Experiments~1 and~3), the PSTD solution agrees with the analytical modal reference to nearly machine precision ($10^{-15}$ to $10^{-14}$) regardless of whether physical damping is present. This confirms that the modal transition matrix $S_m = e^{A_m \Delta t}$ eliminates temporal discretization error for the retained modes. In contrast, the FDTD scheme introduces second-order algebraic truncation errors ($O(\Delta t^2 + \Delta x^2)$), requiring substantial grid refinement to achieve comparable accuracy.}

    \item \textcolor{red}{\textbf{Numerical dispersion and wavepacket transport}: In the propagating pulse benchmarks (Experiments~5 and~6), the PSTD solver exhibits negligible numerical dispersion for the resolved spatial frequencies, transporting wavepackets across repeated boundary reflections with faithful preservation of both amplitude and phase. In contrast, standard FDTD introduces phase-velocity errors that produce spurious high-frequency ripples trailing behind sharp wavefronts (visible in the spacetime plots of Figures~\ref{fig:exp5_spacetime} and~\ref{fig:exp6_spacetime}).}

    \item \textcolor{red}{\textbf{The regularizing impact of viscoelastic damping}: The comparative convergence rates between undamped and damped configurations illustrate the effect of frequency-dependent dissipation for the selected parameters and observation times. In the tested damped non-smooth case (Experiment~4), FDTD attains an observed convergence rate of approximately second order ($\approx 2.22$); this finite-grid rate is specific to that configuration and is not a general asymptotic result.}

    \item \textcolor{red}{\textbf{Trade-offs in computational complexity and geometric flexibility}: For the resolved spatial modes in these benchmarks, PSTD exhibits low numerical dispersion and uses analytical temporal modal propagation. Its DCT formulation is directly suited to Cartesian subdomains with homogeneous Neumann boundary conditions. Evaluating the DCT incurs an $\mathcal{O}(N \log N)$ computational cost per transform, compared to the strictly local $\mathcal{O}(N)$ stencil updates of explicit FDTD. Local geometric features, complex boundaries, and spatially varying medium parameters are not straightforward to accommodate in the global spectral basis. This trade-off motivates the domain decomposition and Adaptive Rectangular Decomposition (ARD) frameworks developed in Chapter~3, which partition the domain into rectangular blocks and exchange interface data.}
\end{itemize}

\chapter{Acceleration of the Numerical Solvers and Inverse Problems}
\label{ch:acceleration}

\section{Optimized Schwarz Waveform Relaxation}
\label{sec:oswr}

To solve the damped wave equation on large computational domains, we employ an overlapping Schwarz Waveform Relaxation (SWR) method. \textcolor{red}{The formulation studied here uses Robin-like transmission conditions parameterized by $q$ and $r$; the OSWR parameters are selected numerically to reduce the convergence factor over a sampled frequency set.} As the PSTD method only supports homogeneous Neumann conditions, we employ SWR only for FDTD.

\subsection{SWR Algorithm}
\textcolor{red}{As is standard in domain decomposition theory \citep{quarteroni_domain_1999,gander_optimized_2007,dolean_introduction_2015}, our analysis considers a canonical two-subdomain configuration to derive its frequency-dependent convergence factor and select transmission parameters. This isolates the effect of the transmission operator without introducing additional interfaces or partition-graph choices; it is not a comparison of the optimal number of subdomains. Extending the analysis to three or more subdomains requires accounting for their interface coupling. Non-overlapping formulations require their own transmission-condition analysis and are not compared here. The overlap $\delta=b-a>0$ affects information transfer between subdomains and can improve convergence; the size of that effect depends on the equation and configuration.}
Let $\Omega=(0,L)$ be decomposed into the two overlapping subdomains $\Omega_1=(0,b)$ and $\Omega_2=(a,L)$, with
\[
0<a<b<L,
\qquad \text{and} \qquad
\delta=b-a>0,
\]
where $\delta$ denotes the overlap width. Let $u$ and $v$ denote the solutions defined on the left ($\Omega_1$) and right ($\Omega_2$) subdomains, respectively.

At Schwarz iteration $k+1$, the subdomain problems are

\begin{align*}
u_{tt}^{(k+1)}
+\gamma u_t^{(k+1)}
&=
c^2u_{xx}^{(k+1)}
+\nu u_{xxt}^{(k+1)}
+f,
&&x\in(0,b),
\\
v_{tt}^{(k+1)}
+\gamma v_t^{(k+1)}
&=
c^2v_{xx}^{(k+1)}
+\nu v_{xxt}^{(k+1)}
+f,
&&x\in(a,L),
\end{align*}

subject to the physical boundary conditions at $x=0$ and $x=L$, together with Robin transmission conditions at the interfaces

\begin{align*}
u_x^{(k+1)}(b,t)
+
q\,u_t^{(k+1)}(b,t)
+
r\,u^{(k+1)}(b,t)
&=
v_x^{(k)}(b,t)
+
q\,v_t^{(k)}(b,t)
+
r\,v^{(k)}(b,t),
\\
-v_x^{(k+1)}(a,t)
+
q\,v_t^{(k+1)}(a,t)
+
r\,v^{(k+1)}(a,t)
&=
-u_x^{(k)}(a,t)
+
q\,u_t^{(k)}(a,t)
+
r\,u^{(k)}(a,t).
\end{align*}

The real numbers $q,r\ge0$ are the transmission parameters that we want to optimize.

\subsection{Convergence Factor}

To analyze the convergence of the algorithm, it is sufficient to consider the error equations. Since the exact solution satisfies the same equations, the forcing term, initial conditions and physical boundary data vanish. We therefore consider

\begin{equation*}
u_{tt}
+\gamma u_t
=
c^2u_{xx}
+\nu u_{xxt}, \qquad x \in (0,L), \ t > 0,
\end{equation*}

with homogeneous Neumann boundary conditions.

Applying the Laplace transform in time gives

\begin{equation*}
(c^2+\nu s)\widehat{u}_{xx}
=
(s^2+\gamma s)\widehat{u}, \qquad s \in \mathbb{C}, \ x \in (0,L).
\end{equation*}

Introducing the Laplace-domain wavenumber

\begin{equation*}
k(s)
=
\sqrt{
\frac{-s^2-\gamma s}
     {c^2+\nu s}
},
\end{equation*}

the transformed equation becomes

\begin{equation*}
\widehat{u}_{xx}
+
k(s)^2
\widehat{u}
=
0.
\end{equation*}

Restricting the Laplace variable to the imaginary axis,
\[
s=i\omega,
\]
recovers the continuous dispersion relation derived in
Section~\ref{sec:dispersion_analysis}, since
\[
k(i\omega)
=
\sqrt{
\frac{\omega^2-i\gamma\omega}
     {c^2+i\nu\omega}
}.
\]

The Laplace symbol of the Robin operator is

\begin{equation*}
\Lambda(s)=qs+r.
\end{equation*}

The homogeneous Neumann conditions at the physical boundaries constrain the general exponential solutions of
\[
\widehat{u}_{xx}+k^2\widehat{u}=0,
\qquad
\widehat{v}_{xx}+k^2\widehat{v}=0
\]
on each subdomain. Writing the general solution as a superposition of counter-propagating exponentials,
\begin{align*}
\widehat{u}(x,s) &= \alpha_u\,e^{ikx} + \beta_u\,e^{-ikx}, \\
\widehat{v}(x,s) &= \alpha_v\,e^{ik(L-x)} + \beta_v\,e^{-ik(L-x)},
\end{align*}
with derivatives
\[
\widehat{u}_x(x,s) = ik\left(\alpha_u e^{ikx}-\beta_u e^{-ikx}\right),
\qquad
\widehat{v}_x(x,s) = -ik\left(\alpha_v e^{ik(L-x)}-\beta_v e^{-ik(L-x)}\right).
\]
Imposing $\widehat u_x(0,s)=0$, $\widehat v_x(L,s)=0$ forces $\beta_u=\alpha_u$ and $\beta_v=\alpha_v$. Absorbing the amplitude into a single constant on each subdomain gives

\begin{align*}
\widehat{u}(x,s) &= A_u\left(e^{ikx}+e^{-ikx}\right), \\
\widehat{v}(x,s) &= A_v\left(e^{ik(L-x)}+e^{-ik(L-x)}\right),
\end{align*}

with corresponding derivatives

\begin{align*}
\widehat{u}_x(x,s) &= ikA_u\left(e^{ikx}-e^{-ikx}\right), \\
\widehat{v}_x(x,s) &= -ikA_v\left(e^{ik(L-x)}-e^{-ik(L-x)}\right).
\end{align*}

Using $L-a=b$ and $L-b=a$, the interface traces are

\begin{align*}
\widehat{u}(a) &= A_u\left(e^{ika}+e^{-ika}\right),
&
\widehat{u}_x(a) &= ikA_u\left(e^{ika}-e^{-ika}\right),
\\
\widehat{u}(b) &= A_u\left(e^{ikb}+e^{-ikb}\right),
&
\widehat{u}_x(b) &= ikA_u\left(e^{ikb}-e^{-ikb}\right),
\\
\widehat{v}(a) &= A_v\left(e^{ikb}+e^{-ikb}\right),
&
\widehat{v}_x(a) &= -ikA_v\left(e^{ikb}-e^{-ikb}\right),
\\
\widehat{v}(b) &= A_v\left(e^{ika}+e^{-ika}\right),
&
\widehat{v}_x(b) &= -ikA_v\left(e^{ika}-e^{-ika}\right).
\end{align*}

Substituting into the Laplace-transformed Robin transmission conditions, we get that

\begin{align*}
(\partial_x+\Lambda)\widehat{u}^{(k+1)}(b)
&=
(\partial_x+\Lambda)\widehat{v}^{(k)}(b),
\\
(-\partial_x+\Lambda)\widehat{v}^{(k+1)}(a)
&=
(-\partial_x+\Lambda)\widehat{u}^{(k)}(a),
\end{align*}

gives

\begin{align*}
A_u^{(k+1)}
\Bigl[
ik\left(e^{ikb}-e^{-ikb}\right)
+\Lambda\left(e^{ikb}+e^{-ikb}\right)
\Bigr]
&=
A_v^{(k)}
\Bigl[
-ik\left(e^{ika}-e^{-ika}\right)
+\Lambda\left(e^{ika}+e^{-ika}\right)
\Bigr],
\\
A_v^{(k+1)}
\Bigl[
ik\left(e^{ikb}-e^{-ikb}\right)
+\Lambda\left(e^{ikb}+e^{-ikb}\right)
\Bigr]
&=
A_u^{(k)}
\Bigl[
-ik\left(e^{ika}-e^{-ika}\right)
+\Lambda\left(e^{ika}+e^{-ika}\right)
\Bigr].
\end{align*}

Denoting the bracketed quantities by
\[
F(b) = ik\left(e^{ikb}-e^{-ikb}\right)+\Lambda\left(e^{ikb}+e^{-ikb}\right),
\; \text{and} \;
H(a) = -ik\left(e^{ika}-e^{-ika}\right)+\Lambda\left(e^{ika}+e^{-ika}\right),
\]
the two equations decouple into a two-step recurrence
\begin{equation*}
A_u^{(k+2)} = G(s)^2 A_u^{(k)},
\qquad
G(s) = \frac{H(a)}{F(b)}.
\end{equation*}

We now pass from exponentials to hyperbolic functions using
\[
e^{ikz}-e^{-ikz}=2\sinh(ikz),
\qquad
e^{ikz}+e^{-ikz}=2\cosh(ikz),
\]
which gives
\begin{align*}
F(b) &= 2\left[ik\sinh(ikb)+\Lambda\cosh(ikb)\right], \\
H(a) &= 2\left[-ik\sinh(ika)+\Lambda\cosh(ika)\right].
\end{align*}

Hence, the convergence factor is

\begin{equation*}
\rho(s)
=
|G(s)|^2
=
\left|
\frac{
ik(s)\sinh\bigl(ik(s)a\bigr)
-
\Lambda(s)\cosh\bigl(ik(s)a\bigr)
}{
ik(s)\sinh\bigl(ik(s)b\bigr)
+
\Lambda(s)\cosh\bigl(ik(s)b\bigr)
}
\right|^2.
\end{equation*}

To obtain optimized transmission conditions, we restrict the Laplace
variable to the imaginary axis, $s=i\omega$, corresponding to purely
oscillatory Fourier modes. On this axis the convergence factor becomes a
function of $\omega$ alone (for fixed $a,b$),

\begin{equation*}
\rho(\omega;q,r)
=
\left|
\frac{
ik(i\omega)\sinh\bigl(ik(i\omega)a\bigr)
-
\Lambda(i\omega)\cosh\bigl(ik(i\omega)a\bigr)
}{
ik(i\omega)\sinh\bigl(ik(i\omega)b\bigr)
+
\Lambda(i\omega)\cosh\bigl(ik(i\omega)b\bigr)
}
\right|^2,
\qquad
\Lambda(i\omega)=iq\omega+r.
\end{equation*}

For the numerical min--max design, we choose the frequency band
\[
\omega\in[\omega_{\min},\omega_{\max}],
\qquad
\omega_{\min}=\frac{\pi}{T},
\qquad
\omega_{\max}=\frac{\pi}{\Delta t}.
\]
The upper endpoint is the temporal Nyquist frequency. The lower cutoff $\pi/T$ is a modelling convention for the finite observation window, not a strict lower bound on the frequencies present in a general transient signal. The objective below therefore optimizes the convergence factor over this chosen band.
\textcolor{blue}{While $\omega_{\max} = \pi/\Delta t$ corresponds to the temporal Nyquist frequency, the lower bound $\omega_{\min} = \pi/T$ represents a modelling choice reflecting the lowest transient frequency component that can complete a half-cycle over the time window $[0,T]$ (in contrast to the fundamental periodic angular frequency $2\pi/T$). Here $\rho=|G|^2$ is the contraction factor over the two-iteration recurrence $A_u^{(k+2)}=G^2A_u^{(k)}$. In the MATLAB implementation, we sample the band at 4,000 logarithmically spaced frequencies and minimize the sampled maximum using the constrained \texttt{patternsearch} optimizer over $q,r\ge0$, initialized at $(q_0,r_0)=(1/c,\gamma/(2c))$. Thus, the reported factors are numerical minima over the sampled objective; they do not certify the continuous-frequency maximum, and the implementation is not a dedicated equioscillation algorithm.}

\begin{equation}
\operatorname*{min}_{q,r\ge0}
\;
\max_{\omega\in[\omega_{\min},\omega_{\max}]}
\rho(\omega;q,r).
\label{eq:minmax}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Numerical Results}

We employ the monolithic FDTD scheme introduced in the previous chapter. To compare the predicted convergence factors for the Optimized Schwarz Waveform Relaxation (OSWR) method with measured iteration behavior, we evaluate the $L^\infty$ domain decomposition error (defined as the maximum absolute difference between the SWR solution and the monolithic FDTD reference solution) over successive Schwarz iterations across the six benchmark configurations defined in Section~\ref{sec:benchmark_problems}.

The domain $\Omega=(0,L)$ is split into two overlapping subdomains with an overlap size $\delta=L/10$. The physical parameters, grid resolution ($\Delta x=0.05$, $\Delta t=0.05$), and total simulation duration ($T=2$) are identical to those defined in the baseline configurations. It is important to note that for spacetime domain decomposition methods like SWR, the total number of iterations required to reach convergence inherently depends on this simulation duration $T$, as boundary information must propagate back and forth across the entire spacetime interface.

We compare the convergence of the SWR iteration using classical Robin transmission conditions ($q=1/c$, $r=0$) against the parameters selected by the sampled-frequency min--max problem (Equation~\ref{eq:minmax}). Figure~\ref{fig:oswr_convergence} illustrates the $L^\infty$ error over successive Schwarz iterations, measured against the monolithic FDTD reference solution. \textcolor{blue}{In these baseline configurations, the sampled-frequency optimized parameters yield lower predicted factors, while the measured acceleration in error contraction is modest. The subsequent experiments show larger measured acceleration in the tested higher-damping and multidimensional cases. These comparisons do not establish a general causal relationship between damping, dimension, and acceleration.}

\begin{figure}[H]
     \begin{subfigure}[b]{0.48\textwidth} \centering \includegraphics[width=0.9\textwidth]{Images/fdtd_snapshots/experiment_1_swr/swr_convergence.png} \caption{Smooth standing wave (undamped)} \end{subfigure}
    \hfill
    \begin{subfigure}[b]{0.48\textwidth} \centering \includegraphics[width=0.9\textwidth]{Images/fdtd_snapshots/experiment_3_swr/swr_convergence.png} \caption{Smooth standing wave (damped)} \end{subfigure} \\
    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \begin{subfigure}[b]{0.48\textwidth} \centering \includegraphics[width=0.9\textwidth]{Images/fdtd_snapshots/experiment_2_swr/swr_convergence.png} \caption{Non-smooth standing wave (undamped)} \end{subfigure}
    \hfill
    \begin{subfigure}[b]{0.48\textwidth} \centering \includegraphics[width=0.9\textwidth]{Images/fdtd_snapshots/experiment_4_swr/swr_convergence.png} \caption{Non-smooth standing wave (damped)} \end{subfigure} \\
    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \begin{subfigure}[b]{0.48\textwidth} \centering \includegraphics[width=0.9\textwidth]{Images/fdtd_snapshots/experiment_5_swr/swr_convergence.png} \caption{Smooth propagating pulse (undamped) \\ ~} \end{subfigure}
    \hfill
    \begin{subfigure}[b]{0.48\textwidth} \centering \includegraphics[width=0.9\textwidth]{Images/fdtd_snapshots/experiment_6_swr/swr_convergence.png} \caption{Non-smooth propagating pulse (undamped)} \end{subfigure}
    \caption{Convergence of the SWR iteration for classical and sampled-frequency optimized Robin transmission conditions under six initial conditions. The optimized parameters reduce the predicted factor on the sampled frequency sets; the plotted runs compare their empirical error histories with the classical choice $q=1/c$, $r=0$.}
    \label{fig:oswr_convergence}
\end{figure}

To better characterize the performance of the Optimized Schwarz Waveform Relaxation method, we investigate the influence of the viscoelastic damping parameter $\nu$ on the optimized Robin parameters. For these evaluations, we consider the non-smooth standing wave configuration as our baseline setup. The simulation duration is set to $T=2$.

Figure~\ref{fig:oswr_nu_sweep} illustrates the behavior of the SWR convergence across varying levels of viscosity $\nu$. As the viscosity increases, the numerically selected transmission parameters deviate from the classical Robin conditions ($q=1/c$, $r=0$). \textcolor{blue}{Figure~\ref{fig:oswr_optimized_params} displays the parameters $q^\ast$ and $r^\ast$ selected by the sampled-frequency min--max search, together with the corresponding predicted factor $\rho_{\mathrm{opt}}$. In the tested parameter range, the sampled-frequency factors remain below one. Since the search uses a finite frequency set, this result does not establish a bound on the continuous-frequency maximum.}

\subsection{Extension to Two-Dimensional Domains}

For a rectangular two-dimensional problem partitioned along the $x$-axis, separation of variables in the transverse direction reduces each compatible transverse eigenmode to an equation in $x$. \textcolor{blue}{We consider the damped wave equation on $\Omega = (0, L_x) \times (0, L_y)$ and two overlapping subdomains $\Omega_1 = (0, b) \times (0, L_y)$ and $\Omega_2 = (a, L_x) \times (0, L_y)$, with $0 < a < b < L_x$ and overlap $\delta=b-a>0$.}

The error equations for the 2D problem are given by
\begin{equation*}
u_{tt} + \gamma u_t = c^2 (u_{xx} + u_{yy}) + \nu (u_{xxt} + u_{yyt}), \qquad (x,y) \in \Omega, \ t > 0.
\end{equation*}

Expanding in transverse eigenfunctions with eigenvalue $k_y^2$ and taking a Laplace transform in time (with complex frequency $s$), the transformed error equation reduces to an ordinary differential equation in $x$:
\begin{equation*}
(s^2 + \gamma s) \hat{u} = (c^2 + \nu s) (\hat{u}_{xx} - k_y^2 \hat{u}).
\end{equation*}
Rearranging the terms, we obtain the characteristic equation governing the spatial modes along the decomposition axis:
\begin{equation*}
\hat{u}_{xx} = K(s, k_y)^2 \hat{u},
\end{equation*}
where the modified dispersion relation is defined as
\begin{equation*}
K(s, k_y) = \sqrt{ \frac{s^2 + \gamma s}{c^2 + \nu s} + k_y^2 }.
\end{equation*}

For each transverse mode, the $x$-dependent equation has the same form as the one-dimensional transformed equation with a modified spatial exponent $K(s,k_y)$. This provides a modewise route to a two-dimensional convergence-factor analysis, with the precise factor depending on the longitudinal boundary conditions. In the numerical experiment below, the script selects parameters by minimizing the sampled overlapping half-space factor
\[
\rho_{\mathrm{hs}}(\omega,k_y;q,r)
=\left|\frac{\Lambda(i\omega)-i k_x}{\Lambda(i\omega)+i k_x}
\,e^{-i k_x\delta}\right|,
\qquad
k_x=\sqrt{\frac{\omega^2-i\gamma\omega}{c^2+i\nu\omega}-k_y^2},
\qquad
\Lambda(i\omega)=r+iq\omega,
\]
over finite temporal and transverse frequency grids. This predictor omits reflections from the finite outer $x$ boundaries of the Dirichlet time-domain problem, so it is used as a parameter-selection indicator and assessed separately against the measured SWR error. The finite search does not prove a continuous-band bound or convergence for arbitrary multidimensional geometries.

\textcolor{blue}{To assess this sampled parameter choice, we implement the overlapping decomposition for FDTD on a 2D Cartesian grid with homogeneous Dirichlet exterior boundaries on $\partial\Omega$, using a multimode sine initial condition. The subdomains exchange data via the second-order BDF Robin discretization applied independently along each row of the artificial interfaces. The experiment uses $\Omega = [0, \pi] \times [0, \pi]$, a $30 \times 30$ grid, and overlap $\delta=2\Delta x$. The tested dissipative parameters are $\nu=1.0$ and $\gamma=0.5$.}

Figure~\ref{fig:oswr_convergence_comparison_2d} tracks the global $L_\infty$ error as a function of Schwarz iteration count for the classical Robin conditions ($q=1/c,r=0$) and the numerically selected parameters $q^\ast,r^\ast$. In this tested 30-by-30 configuration, the selected parameters produce faster error reduction; this result is specific to the chosen grid and physical parameters.
\begin{figure}[H]
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=0.75\textwidth]{Images/fdtd_snapshots/swr_parameters/swr_convergence_comparison_2d.png}
    \caption{SWR iteration error for the tested 2D configuration with classical and sampled-frequency optimized Robin parameters.}
    \label{fig:oswr_convergence_comparison_2d}
\end{figure}


\section{Adaptive Rectangular Decomposition}
\label{sec:ard}

In the previous section, OSWR reduced the FDTD iteration error in the tested configurations, with the size of the improvement varying across cases. The local transmission-condition formulation used there does not transfer directly to the PSTD solver, whose subdomain evolution is represented in a global modal basis.

In contrast, the ARD framework can be formulated with either FDTD or PSTD subdomain solvers. It does not require transmission conditions to be imposed directly at the artificial interfaces: the subdomains evolve under homogeneous Neumann conditions and a localized correction approximates the missing coupling. In this section, we introduce the continuous interpretation and splitting framework. \textcolor{red}{The implementation and numerical evaluation in this thesis use PSTD within the subdomains; FDTD is discussed only as a possible adaptation, with its practical challenges.}

\subsection{A Continuous Interpretation of ARD}

To understand the ARD coupling mechanism, we turn to the immersed-layer distributional framework introduced in~\cite{eldredge_method_2022}. This framework provides a rigorous way to interpret discrete domain decomposition methods by formulating them as a single continuous partial differential equation across the entire domain, using singular distributions (such as Dirac deltas and their derivatives) to model the jump conditions and coupling forces at the artificial interfaces. For simplicity, consider the one-dimensional domain
\[
\Omega=[0,L]
\]
split into two non-overlapping subdomains
\[
\Omega_1=[0,L/2],\qquad
\Omega_2=[L/2,L],
\]
separated by the artificial interface
\[
\Gamma=\{L/2\}.
\]

Let $u$ denote the exact continuous solution. To formalize the decomposition, we introduce the overline notation $\overline{(\cdot)}$ to denote the piecewise assembly of a function or its classical derivatives from the independent subdomain solutions. Thus, $\bar{u}$ is the piecewise field obtained by solving independently on $\Omega_1$ and $\Omega_2$, while $\overline{u_x}$ and $\overline{u_{xx}}$ represent the piecewise fields constructed from their classical first and second spatial derivatives, respectively. Conversely, a derivative subscript applied outside the bar, such as $\bar{u}_x$ or $\bar{u}_{xx}$, denotes the global distributional derivative of the assembled piecewise field $\bar{u}$ over the entire domain $\Omega$. By construction, homogeneous Neumann boundary conditions $\partial_x u = 0$ are imposed on the artificial interface $\Gamma$.

Under this framework, taking the first distributional derivative of the piecewise field yields $\bar{u}_x = \overline{u_x} + [u]\delta_\Gamma$, where $[\cdot]_\Gamma$ denotes the jump operator across the interface $\Gamma$ applied to the assembled field, and $\delta_\Gamma$ is the Dirac delta distribution centered at $\Gamma$. Taking the distributional derivative a second time results in the identity
\[
\bar u_{xx}
=
\overline{u_{xx}}
+
[\partial_xu]\delta_\Gamma
+
[u]\delta_\Gamma',
\]
where $\delta_\Gamma'$ represents the distributional derivative of the Dirac delta. Since the Neumann interface condition enforces
\[
[\partial_xu]=0,
\]
this simplifies to
\begin{equation}
\bar u_{xx}
=
\overline{u_{xx}}
+
[u]\delta_\Gamma'.
\label{eq:dist_deriv}
\end{equation}

Because the Neumann interface condition holds at all times, the temporal derivative also satisfies $[\partial_x u_t]=0$, meaning the exact same distributional identity applies to the mixed viscous term:
\begin{equation*}
\bar u_{txx}
=
\overline{u_{txx}}
+
[u_t]\delta_\Gamma'.
\end{equation*}

Consequently, the full viscoelastic wave equation over the decomposed domain becomes
\begin{equation*}
\bar u_{tt}
+
\gamma\bar u_t
-
\nu\overline{u_{txx}}
=
c^2\overline{u_{xx}}
+
c^2[u]\delta_\Gamma'
+
\nu[u_t]\delta_\Gamma'.
\end{equation*}

Therefore, the independent subdomain solutions satisfy the original wave equation everywhere except at the interface $\Gamma$, where the missing physical coupling appears as a sum of two singular dipole distributions: a stiffness jump ($c^2[u]$) and a viscous jump ($\nu[u_t]$). Recovering the exact monolithic solution is therefore equivalent to explicitly restoring this combined singular interface source, effectively enforcing the continuity of the fields in the subdomains across $\Gamma$.

\subsection{A General Strang Splitting Formulation}

To implement the ARD coupling efficiently, we must first translate the continuous singular dipole sources into a discrete algebraic operator. We define the residual matrix
\begin{equation*}
C_{\mathrm{res}}
=
L_h
-
L_h^{(\mathrm N)},
\end{equation*}
where $L_h$ denotes the interior finite-difference Laplacian crossing the artificial interface, while $L_h^{(\mathrm N)}$ denotes the broken operator obtained by imposing homogeneous Neumann conditions on each subdomain (as introduced in \eqref{eq:boundary_corrected_laplacian}).

This relationship can be directly justified by recalling the distributional identity from \eqref{eq:dist_deriv}. In the discrete setting, applying the global finite-difference Laplacian $L_h \mathbf{u}$ natively evaluates the full distributional derivative $\bar{u}_{xx}$ by differencing across the interface. Conversely, the broken Neumann operator $L_h^{(\mathrm N)} \mathbf{u}$ acts entirely within the isolated subdomains, effectively evaluating only the piecewise classical derivative $\overline{u_{xx}}$. Taking the difference between these two discrete operators isolates the singular distribution:
\[
\underbrace{(L_h - L_h^{(\mathrm N)})}_{C_{\mathrm{res}}} \mathbf{u} \approx \bar{u}_{xx} - \overline{u_{xx}} = [u]\delta_\Gamma'.
\]
\textcolor{red}{Consequently, $C_{\mathrm{res}}$ is motivated as a discrete approximation to the singular dipole contribution. Applying this residual matrix gives localized interface forces, but does not reproduce the continuous distribution exactly and is not the exact residual of the monolithic PSTD spatial operator.}

With this operator defined, we cast the governing PDE as a first-order system in time. Defining the global state vector $\mathbf Z=[\mathbf u;\mathbf v]^T$, the full viscoelastic wave equation can be separated into two distinct evolution operators:
\begin{equation*}
\frac{d\mathbf Z}{dt}
=
\underbrace{
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{local}}(\mathbf Z)
}_{\text{Drift (Local Subdomains)}}
+
\underbrace{
\begin{bmatrix}
0\\
c^2C_{\mathrm{res}}\mathbf u+\nu C_{\mathrm{res}}\mathbf v
\end{bmatrix}
}_{\text{Kick (Interface Correction)}}.
\end{equation*}

The Drift operator $\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{local}}(\mathbf Z)$ abstractly governs the uncoupled physics of the isolated subdomains under homogeneous Neumann boundary conditions, driven by the external physical source. We can treat the time integration of this local evolution as a generic black-box solver $\mathcal S_{\mathrm{local}}(\Delta t)$. 
The Kick operator exclusively applies the localized interface correction forces required to restore the global monolithic solution.

Because the interface correction depends on the instantaneous state, we decouple the operators using a symmetric Kick-Drift-Kick composition patterned after Strang splitting \textcolor{red}{\citep{strang_construction_1968}},
\begin{equation*}
e^{\Delta t(A+B)}
=
e^{\frac{\Delta t}{2}B}
e^{\Delta t A}
e^{\frac{\Delta t}{2}B}
+
\mathcal O(\Delta t^3).
\end{equation*}

During the Drift step, the subdomains evolve independently using the black-box solver. During the Kick step, the positions $\mathbf u$ remain constant, while the velocities satisfy the localized interface ODE:
\begin{equation*}
\mathbf{\dot v}
=
\nu C_{\mathrm{res}}\mathbf v
+
c^2C_{\mathrm{res}}\mathbf u.
\end{equation*}

\textcolor{red}{Because $\mathbf u$ is constant during this substep, this ODE can be solved analytically over a time interval $\tau$:
\begin{equation*}
\mathbf u(t+\tau) = \mathbf u(t),
\qquad
\mathbf v(t+\tau) = e^{\tau\nu C_{\mathrm{res}}}\mathbf v(t) + \frac{c^2}{\nu}\left(e^{\tau\nu C_{\mathrm{res}}} - I\right)\mathbf u(t).
\end{equation*}
In the undamped limit ($\nu \to 0$), the velocity flow reduces to $\mathbf v(t+\tau) = \mathbf v(t) + \tau c^2 C_{\mathrm{res}}\mathbf u(t)$. In this undamped setting, because the Kick matrix $B = \begin{bmatrix} 0 & 0 \\ c^2 C_{\mathrm{res}} & 0 \end{bmatrix}$ satisfies $B^2 = 0$, the forward Euler discretization of the Kick subproblem is the exact flow, and the Kick-Drift-Kick composition rigorously achieves second-order Strang splitting accuracy.}

\textcolor{red}{For the general viscoelastic problem with $\nu > 0$, however, $B = \begin{bmatrix} 0 & 0 \\ c^2 C_{\mathrm{res}} & \nu C_{\mathrm{res}} \end{bmatrix}$ satisfies $B^2 \neq 0$. To avoid the computational cost of evaluating the matrix exponential $e^{\tau\nu C_{\mathrm{res}}}$, we evaluate the Kick step using an explicit forward Euler update:
\begin{equation}
\mathbf u(t+\tau)=\mathbf u(t),
\qquad
\mathbf v(t+\tau)
=
\mathbf v(t)
+
\tau c^2C_{\mathrm{res}}\mathbf u(t)
+
\tau \nu C_{\mathrm{res}}\mathbf v(t).
\label{eq:kick_flow}
\end{equation}
Because the forward Euler approximation introduces a $B^2$ contribution with coefficient $1/4$ instead of the exact coefficient $1/2$ at order $\Delta t^2$, the composition with forward Euler Kick substeps is formally first-order accurate in time for the damped interface operator.}

\textcolor{red}{For the isolated viscous half-kick, a homogeneous velocity perturbation is updated by $\delta\mathbf{v}^{n+1} = (I + \tau\nu C_{\mathrm{res}})\delta\mathbf{v}^{n}$. The usual forward-Euler parabolic stability estimate requires $C_{\mathrm{res}}$ to have real non-positive eigenvalues. We tested the actual matrices returned by \texttt{get\_residue\_matrix} for interface orders $2,4,6,8$ and grid sizes $N_x=20,40,80,160,320,640,800$ by computing their full spectra; the reproducible script is provided in the accompanying code. All tested eigenvalues were real to numerical precision (maximum imaginary part zero at the reported precision), but non-positivity held only for order 2. For orders 4, 6, and 8, the largest eigenvalues were approximately $1.04\times10^{-2}$, $8.30\times10^{-3}$, and $2.91\times10^{-3}$, respectively, independent of the tested $N_x$. Hence the isolated viscous kick is not dissipative for these higher-order residual stencils, and the conditional Gershgorin argument does not establish a general parabolic stability bound for the implemented method. Stability of the full split step must instead be assessed from its complete amplification matrix; the numerical CFL study below addresses that combined update for the configurations tested.}

\subsection{FDTD Context and Scope}

For context, the undamped FDTD leapfrog update can be written in the staggered-velocity form used by velocity Verlet. This equivalence illustrates a possible connection to the splitting framework; it is not a developed or implemented FDTD-based ARD solver. A complete FDTD particularization would also need a local Drift step that enforces the subdomain boundary conditions and is compatible with the Kick state updates.

{\color{red}
For the purely undamped wave equation, the standard explicit leapfrog
FDTD scheme \citep[cfr.][]{bilbao_numerical_2009} is algebraically equivalent to the staggered-velocity
formulation of the second-order Velocity Verlet integrator
\citep{swope1982computer}, making the Strang-split ARD integration straightforward. Indeed, after spatial
discretization, the wave equation can be written as
\begin{equation}
	\ddot{\mathbf{u}}_h = \mathcal{L}_h \mathbf{u}_h,
\end{equation}
where $\mathcal{L}_h$ denotes the discrete spatial wave operator.
Introducing the velocity variable
$\mathbf{v}_h = \dot{\mathbf{u}}_h$, we obtain the first-order system
\begin{equation}
	\begin{cases}
		\dot{\mathbf{u}}_h = \mathbf{v}_h,\\
		\dot{\mathbf{v}}_h = \mathcal{L}_h\mathbf{u}_h.
	\end{cases}
\end{equation}
Applying the Velocity Verlet scheme gives
\begin{equation}
	\begin{aligned}
		\mathbf{v}_h^{n+\frac{1}{2}}
		&= \mathbf{v}_h^n
		+ \frac{\Delta t}{2}\mathcal{L}_h\mathbf{u}_h^n,\\
		\mathbf{u}_h^{n+1}
		&= \mathbf{u}_h^n
		+ \Delta t\,\mathbf{v}_h^{n+\frac{1}{2}},\\
		\mathbf{v}_h^{n+1}
		&= \mathbf{v}_h^{n+\frac{1}{2}}
		+ \frac{\Delta t}{2}\mathcal{L}_h\mathbf{u}_h^{n+1}.
	\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Equivalently, using a staggered representation of the velocity,
\begin{equation}
	\begin{aligned}
		\mathbf{v}_h^{n+\frac{1}{2}}
		&= \mathbf{v}_h^{n-\frac{1}{2}}
		+ \Delta t\,\mathcal{L}_h\mathbf{u}_h^n,\\
		\mathbf{u}_h^{n+1}
		&= \mathbf{u}_h^n
		+ \Delta t\,\mathbf{v}_h^{n+\frac{1}{2}}.
	\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Eliminating the intermediate velocity yields
\begin{equation}
		\mathbf{u}_h^{n+1}
		-2\mathbf{u}_h^n
		+\mathbf{u}_h^{n-1}
		=
		\Delta t^2\mathcal{L}_h\mathbf{u}_h^n,
\end{equation}
which is precisely the standard explicit leapfrog FDTD
discretization of the undamped wave equation.}

For the viscoelastic equation, the mixed derivative $\mathbf u_{txx}$ can be treated implicitly or semi-implicitly, as in the FDTD scheme developed earlier, or explicitly with a suitable time-step restriction. An FDTD-based ARD method would need to specify how this term is split between the local Drift and interface Kick and analyze the resulting complete update. We do not develop or evaluate that variant here; the following algorithmic development is for PSTD subdomains.

\subsection{Application to PSTD}
\label{sec:pstd_multi_subdomain}

The ARD coupling uses a localized finite-difference approximation to the residual across the artificial interface, while each subdomain interior is propagated pseudo-spectrally. This allows the interface stencil to be designed separately from the interior propagator; its choice still affects coupling accuracy and stability. In this thesis, we implement and study second-, fourth-, sixth-, and eighth-order interface stencils.

For each subdomain, the solution is expanded in the DCT basis, yielding the uncoupled modal state vectors $\mathbf Z_{1,m}$ and $\mathbf Z_{2,m}$. The local Drift operator is simply the exact state-transition matrix $S_m(\Delta t)$ derived in the previous section \eqref{eq:modal_general_update}, incorporating the analytical propagation of the damped modal oscillators and the external physical forcing via the $T_m$ matrix.

{\color{red}
\paragraph{Initialization ($n=0$).}
The time-stepping scheme is initialized from the prescribed physical initial displacement $\mathbf{u}_0$ and velocity $\mathbf{v}_0$ sampled on the discrete grid. The initial modal states $\mathbf{U}_m^0$ and $\mathbf{V}_m^0$ on each subdomain are obtained directly by applying the forward DCT:
\begin{equation*}
    \mathbf{U}_m^0 = \mathcal{D} \{ \mathbf{u}_0 \} \quad \text{and} \quad \mathbf{V}_m^0 = \mathcal{D} \{ \mathbf{v}_0 \},
\end{equation*}
yielding the initial state vector $\mathbf{Z}_m^0 = [\mathbf{U}_m^0, \mathbf{V}_m^0]^T$.}

At each time step $t_n$, the complete ARD-PSTD algorithm executes the following Strang-split sequence:

\begin{enumerate}

\item
\textbf{Interface reconstruction.}
The modal coefficients are transformed to the physical domain via the inverse DCT to obtain $\mathbf u^n$ and $\mathbf v^n$:
\begin{equation*}
    \mathbf{u}^n = \mathcal{D}^{-1} \{ \mathbf{U}_m^n \} \quad \text{and} \quad \mathbf{v}^n = \mathcal{D}^{-1} \{ \mathbf{V}_m^n \}.
\end{equation*}

\item
\textbf{First half-kick.}
The physical velocities are advanced over $\tau = \Delta t/2$ using the explicit Kick update \eqref{eq:kick_flow}. Because $C_{\mathrm{res}}$ acts locally in the physical domain, this evaluation is performed directly on the physical grid:
\begin{equation*}
    \mathbf{v}^{n+1/2} = \mathbf{v}^n + \frac{\Delta t}{2} \left( c^2 C_{\mathrm{res}} \mathbf{u}^n + \nu C_{\mathrm{res}} \mathbf{v}^n \right).
\end{equation*}
The updated velocities are then projected back into modal space via the forward DCT to obtain the intermediate modal states $\mathbf Z_m^{n+1/2}$:
\begin{equation*}
    \mathbf{V}_m^{n+1/2} = \mathcal{D} \{ \mathbf{v}^{n+1/2} \} \quad \text{and} \quad \mathbf{U}_m^{n+1/2} = \mathbf{U}_m^n,
\end{equation*}
yielding $\mathbf{Z}_m^{n+1/2} = [\mathbf{U}_m^{n+1/2}, \mathbf{V}_m^{n+1/2}]^T$.

\item
\textbf{Exact drift.}
The uncoupled modal equations are propagated analytically over a full time step $\Delta t$ with the exact state-transition matrix $S_m(\Delta t)$, yielding the updated intermediate modal states $\mathbf Z_m^*$:
\begin{equation*}
    \mathbf{Z}_m^* = S_m(\Delta t) \mathbf{Z}_m^{n+1/2}.
\end{equation*}

\item
\textbf{Interface reconstruction.}
The updated modal states $\mathbf Z_m^*$ are transformed back to the physical domain via the inverse DCT to obtain $\mathbf u^*$ and $\mathbf v^*$:
\begin{equation*}
    \mathbf{u}^* = \mathcal{D}^{-1} \{ \mathbf{U}_m^* \} \quad \text{and} \quad \mathbf{v}^* = \mathcal{D}^{-1} \{ \mathbf{V}_m^* \}.
\end{equation*}

\item
\textbf{Second half-kick.}
The physical velocities are advanced over the second half-step $\tau = \Delta t/2$ using the explicit Kick update \eqref{eq:kick_flow}:
\begin{equation*}
    \mathbf{v}^{n+1} = \mathbf{v}^* + \frac{\Delta t}{2} \left( c^2 C_{\mathrm{res}} \mathbf{u}^* + \nu C_{\mathrm{res}} \mathbf{v}^* \right).
\end{equation*}
Finally, the completed physical velocities are projected back into modal space to yield $\mathbf Z_m^{n+1}$:
\begin{equation*}
    \mathbf{V}_m^{n+1} = \mathcal{D} \{ \mathbf{v}^{n+1} \} \quad \text{and} \quad \mathbf{U}_m^{n+1} = \mathbf{U}_m^*,
\end{equation*}
yielding the fully synchronized state $\mathbf{Z}_m^{n+1} = [\mathbf{U}_m^{n+1}, \mathbf{V}_m^{n+1}]^T$.

\end{enumerate}

The local modal propagator and localized explicit Kick combine the independent subdomain evolutions with a discrete approximation of the missing interface coupling. This formulation requires no global implicit interface solve. Since the residual is a finite-difference operator, the coupled method does not in general preserve the spectral accuracy of the monolithic PSTD discretization.

\subsection{Stability Considerations}

The uncoupled PSTD modal propagator has no exponentially growing modes, subject to the neutral constant-mode drift described in Proposition~\ref{prop:stability}. This fact alone gives no stability bound for the ARD-PSTD product, which also includes the explicit interface correction. A CFL-type restriction is observed in the finite-dimensional stability sweeps below, but a general bound for the implemented method is not established.

The ARD update is organized as a Kick--Drift--Kick composition. In the
undamped case, the Kick flow is exact because its state matrix is nilpotent,
so this is a symmetric Strang splitting \citep{strang_construction_1968}.
For $\nu>0$, the implemented Kick uses forward Euler and the resulting
damped update is only first-order accurate in time.

\begin{itemize}
    \item the exact evolution of the uncoupled modal oscillators;
    \item the explicit interface correction generated by $C_{\mathrm{res}}$.
\end{itemize}

The first operator is integrated analytically and therefore introduces no time-discretization restriction on its own. The stability of the composition depends on the complete product and on the interaction between the modal Drift and the interface Kick.

An important observation is that the residual matrix
\[
C_{\mathrm{res}}
=
L_h
-
L_h^{(\mathrm N)}
\]
is supported only on a small neighborhood of the artificial interfaces. This locality does not make the stability depend only on the spectrum of $C_{\mathrm{res}}$: the full amplification matrix also depends on the modal Drift and on the non-commuting interaction between the two operators.

Because the exact modal propagator is diagonal in the modal basis whereas the interface correction is sparse in the physical basis, the two operators cannot in general be simultaneously diagonalized. No general scalar formula for the stability threshold is therefore available. For the numerical estimates below, we construct the complete one-step amplification matrix
\[
G
=
K\!\left(\frac{\Delta t}{2}\right)
D(\Delta t)
K\!\left(\frac{\Delta t}{2}\right),
\]
\textcolor{red}{where $K(\Delta t/2)$ denotes the half-step localized interface correction matrix obtained from Equation~\eqref{eq:kick_flow} with $\tau = \Delta t/2$,}
\begin{equation*}
{\color{red}K\!\left(\frac{\Delta t}{2}\right) = \begin{bmatrix} I & 0 \\ \frac{\Delta t}{2} c^2 C_{\mathrm{res}} & I + \frac{\Delta t}{2} \nu C_{\mathrm{res}} \end{bmatrix},}
\end{equation*}
\textcolor{red}{and $D(\Delta t) = \mathcal{P}^T S(\Delta t) \mathcal{P}$ represents the uncoupled modal Drift propagator in physical space, with $\mathcal{P} = \operatorname{diag}(\Phi, \Phi)$ denoting the orthogonal Discrete Cosine Transform and $S(\Delta t) = \operatorname{diag}_{m}(S_m)$ the block-diagonal collection of exact modal transition matrices $S_m = e^{A_m \Delta t}$ defined in \eqref{eq:Sm_def}.} In the numerical study below, a threshold estimate is obtained from the spectral-radius condition
\[
\rho(G)\le1.
\]
This condition is used as a numerical indicator; by itself it does not rule
out defective unit-modulus eigenvalues or transient growth of a non-normal
matrix. The analytic result in Proposition~\ref{prop:ard_order2_stability}
establishes power boundedness only for the stated undamped order-2 case and
the sufficient CFL range given there.

For the undamped second-order interface stencil, a restricted analytic
sufficient condition can nevertheless be proved.
\begin{proposition}[Sufficient stability condition for the undamped order-2 KDK scheme]
\label{prop:ard_order2_stability}
Consider the one-dimensional, cell-centered ARD-PSTD discretization on
$[0,L]$, divided into two equal Neumann subdomains with $N_x/2$ nodes each,
where $N_x$ is even and $\Delta x=L/N_x$. Let $\gamma=\nu=0$ and take zero
external forcing. Use the second-order residual
\[
C_{\mathrm{res}}
=\frac{1}{\Delta x^2}
\begin{bmatrix}
-1&1\\
1&-1
\end{bmatrix}
\]
on the two nodes adjacent to the interface (and zero elsewhere), and use
exact modal Drift with the Kick--Drift--Kick update. If
\[
0<\sigma=\frac{c\Delta t}{\Delta x}\le\frac12,
\]
then the full homogeneous KDK amplification is power bounded on the invariant
subspace orthogonal to the spatially constant displacement and velocity
states. In that two-dimensional constant-in-space subspace, displacement is
neutral when the mean velocity is zero; a nonzero mean velocity produces the
physical linear drift of the spatial mean.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Let $A\succeq0$ be the block-diagonal squared-frequency operator of the
uncoupled Neumann subdomains, $\Omega=A^{1/2}$, and
$B=c^2C_{\mathrm{res}}\preceq0$. Write $h=\Delta t$ and define
$P=\cos(h\Omega)$ and $Q=q(\Omega)$, where
$q(\omega)=\sin(h\omega)/\omega$ for $\omega>0$ and $q(0)=h$. The exact
Drift is
\[
D(h)=
\begin{bmatrix}
P&Q\\
-\Omega\sin(h\Omega)&P
\end{bmatrix},
\]
and the half-Kick is
\[
K(h/2)=
\begin{bmatrix}
I&0\\
hB/2&I
\end{bmatrix}.
\]
Since the largest local modal frequency is
$\Omega_{\max}=c\pi(N_x-2)/L$, the CFL assumption implies
$h\Omega_{\max}<\pi/2$. Thus $Q\succ0$ and $P\succeq0$.

Eliminating the velocity from the KDK update gives the displacement
recurrence
\[
\mathbf u^{n+1}-2M\mathbf u^n+\mathbf u^{n-1}=0,
\qquad
M=P+\frac h2 QB.
\]
The matrix $M$ is similar to the symmetric matrix
\[
\widetilde M=Q^{-1/2}MQ^{1/2}
=P+\frac h2Q^{1/2}BQ^{1/2}.
\]
Consequently, its eigenvalues are real. Since $B\preceq0$,
$\lambda_{\max}(\widetilde M)\le1$. Also
$\|Q\|\le h$ and
$\|B\|=2c^2/\Delta x^2$, so
\[
\lambda_{\min}(\widetilde M)
\ge \cos(h\Omega_{\max})-\frac{h^2}{2}\|B\|
\ge \cos(\pi\sigma)-\sigma^2
\ge -\frac14>-1.
\]
If $\widetilde Mx=x$, then
$x^T(I-P)x=\frac h2x^TQ^{1/2}BQ^{1/2}x$. The left side is non-negative and
the right side is non-positive, so both vanish. Hence $x$ lies in the
zero-frequency subspace of the two local Neumann blocks and
$BQ^{1/2}x=0$. On that subspace the order-2 interface block annihilates only
the equal-constant combination, so $\mu=1$ is the global constant mode alone.
For an eigenvalue $\mu$ of $\widetilde M$, the recurrence has characteristic
polynomial $z^2-2\mu z+1$. Hence all roots lie on the unit circle and are
distinct for $-1<\mu<1$, giving bounded oscillations. Since $Q\succ0$, the
top row of the KDK update gives
\[
\mathbf v^n=Q^{-1}(\mathbf u^{n+1}-M\mathbf u^n),
\]
so bounded displacement sequences also give bounded velocities. The sole
$\mu=1$ eigenvector is the global constant mode, for which the recurrence has
the double root $z=1$ and permits linear mean drift when the mean velocity is
nonzero. Thus the complementary phase-space subspace is power bounded, while
the constant-in-space phase subspace has only the stated physical drift.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Numerical Experiments}

\subsubsection{Stability}

The numerical sweep of the complete one-step amplification matrix suggests that the localized interface correction imposes a modest time-step restriction for the tested cases. Because the explicit correction projects global spectral modes onto a local interface stencil\footnote{\textcolor{red}{In finite-difference methods, a computational stencil refers to the geometric arrangement of neighboring grid points sampled to approximate a differential operator. Here, an \emph{interface stencil} specifically denotes the local finite-difference operator straddling the artificial boundary between adjacent subdomains to evaluate the coupling correction $C_{\text{res}}$.}}, the estimated threshold varies somewhat with grid resolution $N$, consistent with possible discrete resonances between global modes and the interface stencil. \textcolor{red}{We numerically swept the amplification matrix $G$ (the single-step transition combining the modal propagator and interface correction) for grid resolutions up to $N=800$. The estimated maximum Courant numbers for second-, fourth-, sixth-, and eighth-order interface operators (the spatial order of $C_{\text{res}}$) are}
\[
% These asymptotic worst-case bounds are computed using code/pstd/scripts/check_nx_trend.m
\mathrm{CFL}_{\max}
\approx
1.117,\;
1.046,\;
1.017,\;
0.991,
\]
respectively. The estimates decrease with stencil order over the tested configurations. For the eighth-order stencil, the estimate at the largest tested resolution is approximately $0.991$; the finite sweep does not establish an asymptotic worst-case limit. Courant numbers slightly greater than $1$ can occur because the CFL number is normalized by the standard explicit FDTD limit, whereas the analytical modal propagator has no such intrinsic limit.

For the full split update with viscoelastic damping ($\nu > 0$), we also examine the dimensionless number
\[
S = \frac{\nu \Delta t}{\Delta x^2}.
\]
A finite-dimensional numerical search of the full amplification matrix estimates the stable range in $S$ for a specified grid and parameter set. The tested residue matrices have positive eigenvalues for orders 4, 6, and 8, so the isolated forward-Euler diffusion argument does not supply a general bound for those stencils. At $N=200$, $L=c=\nu=1$, and $\gamma=0$, the numerical search returns the following estimates:
\[
S_{\max} =
2.000,\;
1.487,\;
1.318,\;
1.233,
\]
respectively. The second-order estimate agrees with the classical explicit-diffusion value in this setup; the remaining values are numerical results for this grid and parameter choice, not rigorous or mesh-independent bounds.

To compare the undamped hyperbolic CFL estimates with long-run behavior, we simulate a one-dimensional Gaussian pulse with $\nu=\gamma=0$. The domain length is $L=1$, the wave speed is $c=1$, and we use $N=100$ grid points split into two non-overlapping subdomains of $N_{\mathrm{sub}}=50$ points each. The simulation runs to $T=250$ to expose mild exponential growth.

For each spatial order, the script uses its amplification-matrix estimate for $N=100$ and runs one simulation with $\Delta t=(\mathrm{CFL}_{N=100}\mp\epsilon)\Delta x/c$, using $\epsilon=0.05$. We inspect the maximum global wavefield amplitude at the end of each run.

For the tested higher-order cases, the runs below the estimated threshold remain bounded over $T=250$; the second-order case exhibits delayed growth even slightly below its estimate. These finite-duration tests support the undamped threshold estimates for this grid but do not prove stability at arbitrary resolutions or validate the damped $S$ estimates.

% The following empirical divergence amplitudes at T=250 are computed using code/pstd/scripts/verify_cfl_empirical.m
For the runs above the estimated undamped threshold, amplitudes grow substantially over this long interval, reaching floating-point overflow for the sixth- and eighth-order cases. This comparison is numerical evidence for the tested setup, not a proof of a general stability boundary.

\subsubsection{Convergence of the Interface Correction Operator}
\label{sec:accuracy_analysis}

To evaluate the accuracy of the proposed explicit interface correction operator, we quantify the $L^\infty$ domain decomposition error, defined as
\begin{equation*}
E_{\infty} = \max_{j,n} \left| u_{j,n}^{\text{mono}} - u_{j,n}^{\text{ARD}} \right|,
\end{equation*}
as a function of the interface spatial order and grid resolution. The domain $\Omega=(0,L)$ is decomposed into two non-overlapping subdomains, and the ARD interface operator is applied with varying stencil sizes (Order 2, 4, 6, and 8). We evaluate the method across the six benchmark configurations defined in Section~\ref{sec:benchmark_problems}. For the propagating pulses (Configurations V and VI), they are placed asymmetrically at $x=L/4$ to prevent artificial symmetry at the boundary.

Figure~\ref{fig:ard_convergence_dx} and Figure~\ref{fig:ard_convergence_order} present the $L^\infty$ domain decomposition error relative to the monolithic PSTD reference solution as a function of the grid resolution $\Delta x$ and the spatial order, respectively. 

\textcolor{red}{As observed in Figure~\ref{fig:ard_convergence_dx}, which displays quadratic ($O(\Delta x^2)$) and linear ($O(\Delta x)$) reference slopes, the empirical domain decomposition error exhibits different trends across the tested profiles and grid refinements. For the smooth standing-wave configurations (a) and (b), the error decreases approximately quadratically on coarser grids and approaches a linear trend on finer meshes. For the non-smooth configurations (c, d, f) and propagating pulses, the error is predominantly consistent with a linear trend over the tested resolutions. These observations are compatible with contributions from interface truncation and splitting error, but the experiments do not isolate their individual effects or establish a general asymptotic rate.}

\textcolor{red}{Furthermore, Figure~\ref{fig:ard_convergence_order} illustrates the effect of increasing the spatial order of the correction operator for a fixed grid resolution. The higher-order stencils yield lower domain decomposition error in these benchmarks, with modest improvements over the tested orders relative to their increased computational cost. The finite set of tests does not establish an asymptotic rate with respect to stencil order.}

\subsubsection{3D Numerical Experiments}

To demonstrate the capability of the proposed ARD solver in higher dimensions, we simulate the damped wave equation on a 3D computational domain composed of three interconnected rectangular subdomains. The physical dimensions are defined using a uniform spatial discretization step $\Delta h = 0.2$\,m. The three connected regions are parameterized by their grid offsets $(x_0, y_0, z_0)$ and grid dimensions $(N_x, N_y, N_z)$, yielding the sequence of connected subdomains depicted in Figure~\ref{fig:ard_3d_geometry}.

\textcolor{red}{This geometric configuration models a room with a stepped non-convex shape chosen deliberately as a benchmark to evaluate domain decomposition and wave transmission across virtual boundaries. The multi-subdomain formulation can accommodate a class of convex and non-convex orthogonal geometries that can be represented as unions of axis-aligned rectangular subdomains. In this setup, the interior interfaces $\Gamma_1$ and $\Gamma_2$ are entirely artificial boundaries introduced solely to partition the non-rectangular space into canonical rectangular subdomains $\Omega_1, \Omega_2, \Omega_3$ for spectral DCT-II processing. The benchmark specifically tests whether acoustic waves traverse these virtual internal boundaries in a continuous acoustic space without generating spurious numerical reflections or interface artifacts. The exact physical coordinates and grid dimensions of the subdomains are defined as follows:}
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textcolor{red}{Subdomain 1 ($\Omega_1$): $x \in [0, 30]$\,m, $y \in [0, 10]$\,m, $z \in [0, 10]$\,m ($150 \times 50 \times 50$ cells, volume $3000$\,m$^3$).}
    \item \textcolor{red}{Subdomain 2 ($\Omega_2$): $x \in [2, 28]$\,m, $y \in [10, 20]$\,m, $z \in [0, 10]$\,m ($130 \times 50 \times 50$ cells, volume $2600$\,m$^3$).}
    \item \textcolor{red}{Subdomain 3 ($\Omega_3$): $x \in [5, 25]$\,m, $y \in [20, 30]$\,m, $z \in [0, 10]$\,m ($100 \times 50 \times 50$ cells, volume $2000$\,m$^3$).}
\end{itemize}
\textcolor{red}{The total enclosed room volume is $V = 7600$\,m$^3$. The two interior artificial coupling interfaces are located at $\Gamma_1 = [2, 28] \times \{10\} \times [0, 10]$\,m and $\Gamma_2 = [5, 25] \times \{20\} \times [0, 10]$\,m. Exterior boundaries along all walls, floor, and ceiling are perfectly rigid homogeneous Neumann surfaces ($\partial_n p = 0$). No absorbing boundary layers or PML partitions are introduced, establishing an undamped multi-reflection benchmark.}

\textcolor{red}{The global 3D FDTD reference solver operates on the exact domain union $\Omega = \Omega_1 \cup \Omega_2 \cup \Omega_3$ using a second-order standard leapfrog scheme:
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
p^{n+1}_{i,j,k} = 2 p^n_{i,j,k} - p^{n-1}_{i,j,k} &+ \left(\frac{c_0 \Delta t}{\Delta h}\right)^2 \Delta_h p^n_{i,j,k} \\
&+ \frac{\nu \Delta t}{\Delta h^2} \left( \Delta_h p^n_{i,j,k} - \Delta_h p^{n-1}_{i,j,k} \right) + \Delta t^2 S(t^n) \, \delta_{(i,j,k),(i_s,j_s,k_s)},
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where the 3D discrete Laplacian is evaluated with a 7-point stencil:
\begin{equation}
\Delta_h p^n_{i,j,k} = \sum_{(i',j',k') \in \mathcal{N}_{\text{int}}(i,j,k)} \left( p^n_{i',j',k'} - p^n_{i,j,k} \right).
\end{equation}
Here $\mathcal{N}_{\text{int}}(i,j,k)$ denotes the set of orthogonal nearest neighbors that lie strictly within the room interior. Enforcing zero fluxes across boundary faces omitting exterior stencil connections inherently discretizes the homogeneous Neumann condition $\partial_n p = 0$ with second-order accuracy. For the damped scenario, the mixed viscous derivative $\nu \Delta \partial_t p$ is discretized via backward differencing, yielding the explicit damping term above.}

\textcolor{red}{To provide a structural finite element comparison, we also implement a 3D Time-Domain Finite Element Method (TD-FEM) solver on the same domain geometry. In the weak variational formulation of the undamped acoustic wave equation with rigid walls ($\partial_n p = 0$), test functions $w \in H^1(\Omega)$ satisfy:
\begin{equation}
\int_\Omega w \, \ddot{p} \, d\Omega + c_0^2 \int_\Omega \nabla w \cdot \nabla p \, d\Omega = \int_\Omega w \, S(t) \, \delta(\mathbf{x} - \mathbf{x}_s) \, d\Omega.
\end{equation}
Discretizing the domain into eight-node trilinear hexahedral ($Q_1$) brick elements leads to the semi-discrete system
\begin{equation}
M \, \ddot{\mathbf{p}}(t) + c_0^2 K \, \mathbf{p}(t) = \mathbf{f}(t),
\end{equation}
where $M$ is the global mass matrix and $K$ is the global stiffness matrix assembled from analytical element tensor-product matrices. Employing row-sum mass lumping yields a diagonal mass matrix $M_{\mathrm{lump}}$, which enables an explicit leapfrog time-stepping update:
\begin{equation}
\mathbf{p}^{n+1} = 2 \mathbf{p}^n - \mathbf{p}^{n-1} - c_0^2 \Delta t^2 M_{\mathrm{lump}}^{-1} K \mathbf{p}^n + \Delta t^2 M_{\mathrm{lump}}^{-1} \mathbf{f}^n.
\end{equation}
Because the homogeneous Neumann boundary condition is the natural boundary condition of the weak form, boundary nodes inherently satisfy $\partial_n p = 0$ without requiring artificial ghost cells or exterior stencils.}

\textcolor{red}{Figure~\ref{fig:ard_3d_snapshots} illustrates the temporal evolution of the 2D horizontal acoustic pressure field ($z = 3.0$\,m) across three key propagation epochs ($t = 0.03, 0.08, 0.16$\,s):}
\begin{enumerate}
    \item \textcolor{red}{At $t = 0.03$\,s, the expanding wavefront reaches the artificial interface $\Gamma_2$ ($y = 20$\,m) and enters Subdomain 2.}
    \item \textcolor{red}{At $t = 0.08$\,s, the wavefront traverses the second interface $\Gamma_1$ ($y = 10$\,m) into Subdomain 1 and reaches the rigid wall at $y = 0$\,m.}
    \item \textcolor{red}{At $t = 0.16$\,s, the reflection from the far wall propagates across $\Gamma_1$ and $\Gamma_2$ toward Subdomain 3.}
\end{enumerate}
 \textcolor{red}{The displayed snapshots show visually continuous wavefields across the interface lines $\Gamma_1$ and $\Gamma_2$ for this configuration. Visual inspection does not quantify interface reflections or phase errors and cannot establish their absence.}

\textcolor{red}{Figure~\ref{fig:ard_3d_snapshots} compares the qualitative wavefield patterns across the tested solvers and spatial resolutions:}
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textcolor{red}{\textbf{Global 3D FDTD (Figures~\ref{subfig:3d_fdtd_coarse} and~\ref{subfig:3d_fdtd_fine}):} On the coarse grid ($\Delta h = 0.20$\,m, subfigure~(a)), the 7-point Cartesian stencil shows visible phase lag and trailing ripples behind the primary wavefront. These artifacts are less apparent on the refined $\Delta h = 0.10$\,m grid (subfigure~(b)); this is a qualitative visual comparison and does not measure a factor-of-four error reduction.}
    \item \textcolor{red}{\textbf{3D Acoustic TD-FEM (Figures~\ref{subfig:3d_fem_coarse} and~\ref{subfig:3d_fem_fine}):} The 27-point stencil couples each node to its 26 neighbors and is designed to reduce directional bias. In these snapshots, the coarse-grid TD-FEM field appears less directionally biased than coarse FDTD; the fine-grid pattern is smoother. These visual observations do not quantify isotropy or error.}
    \item \textcolor{red}{\textbf{Multi-Subdomain ARD-PSTD (Figure~\ref{subfig:3d_ard_coarse}):} On the coarse grid ($\Delta h = 0.20$\,m, subfigure~(e)), ARD-PSTD produces sharp wavefronts that visually resemble those in the fine-grid reference snapshots. This qualitative comparison is consistent with reduced spatial dispersion for resolved modes in the tested configuration, but does not establish absolute accuracy or isolate the effect of the spectral spatial derivative.}
\end{itemize}

\textcolor{red}{Regarding computational performance, a clear distinction must be drawn between per-step algebraic complexity and global resolution efficiency. In our empirical benchmarks executed on an Intel CPU with AVX2 vectorization, the execution times for $T = 0.24$\,s are measured as follows:}
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textcolor{red}{\textbf{Global 3D FDTD coarse} ($\Delta h = 0.20$\,m, $1200$ steps): $22.1$\,s in multi-thread execution ($8$ cores) and $93.0$\,s in single-thread.}
    \item \textcolor{red}{\textbf{Global 3D FDTD fine} ($\Delta h = 0.10$\,m, $2400$ steps): $92.6$\,s in multi-thread and $390.0$\,s in single-thread.}
    \item \textcolor{red}{\textbf{3D TD-FEM coarse} ($\Delta h = 0.20$\,m, $1200$ steps): $51.6$\,s in multi-thread and $232.0$\,s in single-thread.}
    \item \textcolor{red}{\textbf{3D TD-FEM fine} ($\Delta h = 0.10$\,m, $2400$ steps): $144.3$\,s in multi-thread and $650.0$\,s in single-thread.}
    \item \textcolor{red}{\textbf{Multi-Subdomain ARD-PSTD coarse} ($\Delta h = 0.20$\,m, $1200$ steps): $187.4$\,s in single-thread FFTW execution and approximately $36.0$\,s when utilizing multi-threaded FFTW on $8$ cores.}
\end{itemize}

\textcolor{red}{When evaluated on the exact same spatial grid ($\Delta h = 0.20$\,m), FDTD executes faster per step because it computes a minimal 7-point Cartesian stencil (roughly 10 floating-point operations per cell), whereas ARD-PSTD computes four full 3D forward and inverse DCT-II transforms per step (scaled as $\mathcal{O}(N \log N)$) alongside interface residue exchanges.}

\textcolor{red}{Although ARD-PSTD has a higher computational cost per time step than the local FDTD stencil on an identical grid, its higher spatial accuracy allows a coarser discretization for the frequency range considered. In this benchmark, this translates into lower execution times than the refined FDTD and TD-FEM configurations: multi-threaded ARD-PSTD ($36.0$\,s) is approximately $2.57\times$ faster than fine FDTD ($92.6$\,s) and $4.0\times$ faster than fine TD-FEM ($144.3$\,s). We note that requiring $10\text{--}15$ points per wavelength (PPW) for low-order schemes is an approximate engineering guideline dependent on the required phase tolerance, scheme order, and propagation distance. Further reductions in memory footprint and execution time at finer reference resolutions (such as $\Delta h = 0.05$\,m) are expected from the reduced number of spatial degrees of freedom, but represent extrapolated estimates rather than measured performance.}

\textcolor{red}{To inspect the local acoustic response in the frequency domain, Figure~\ref{fig:ard_3d_receiver} compares the receiver response at $\mathbf{x}_r = (15.0, 15.0, 3.0)$\,m inside Subdomain 2 for lossless propagation ($\nu = 0$). We compare multi-subdomain ARD-PSTD (coarse, $\Delta h = 0.20$\,m) with coarse ($\Delta h = 0.20$\,m) and fine ($\Delta h = 0.10$\,m) configurations of global 3D FDTD and 3D Acoustic TD-FEM. Figure~\ref{fig:ard_3d_receiver} displays the Room Frequency Response Function (FRF / RTF, $|H(f)| = |P(f)/S(f)|$) obtained via regularized spectral deconvolution with the source pulse $S(t)$.}

\textcolor{red}{This multi-resolution spectral comparison supports two observations for the tested configuration:}
\begin{enumerate}
    \item \textcolor{red}{\textbf{Sub-200\,Hz cross-method comparison:} Below $200$\,Hz, the tested coarse and fine solver configurations show close agreement on the discrete acoustic eigenmode frequencies of the stepped enclosure. In particular, both fine TD-FEM and fine FDTD closely match coarse ARD-PSTD in peak positions and relative amplitudes. This is a cross-method consistency check for the tested configuration; without an analytical or experimental reference, it does not establish the absolute accuracy of the reconstructed acoustic field.}
    \item \textcolor{red}{\textbf{Above-200\,Hz Analysis Limitations and Dispersion:} As frequency increases above $200$\,Hz, the coarse FDTD and coarse TD-FEM spectra exhibit downward frequency shifts and modal amplitude discrepancies due to cumulative numerical dispersion. Refining the spatial mesh to $\Delta h = 0.10$\,m brings both FDTD and TD-FEM into substantially closer alignment with ARD-PSTD. However, above $200$\,Hz small variations in peak heights and resonance profiles persist among the formulations. In the absence of an analytical closed-form solution or experimental ground truth for this non-trivial stepped 3D geometry, it cannot be definitively determined which numerical formulation is superior above $200$\,Hz, which represents an acknowledged limitation of this cross-verification analysis. The agreement below $200\,\mathrm{Hz}$ is a cross-method consistency check for the tested configuration; without an analytical or experimental reference, it does not by itself establish absolute accuracy, robustness, or interface stability.}
\end{enumerate}

\section{\textcolor{red}{Differentiable Physics Neural Networks for Inverse Problems}}
\label{sec:dpnn}

\subsection{\textcolor{red}{DPNN Architecture and Optimization Workflow}}
\label{subsec:dpnn_arch}

\textcolor{red}{In this section, we introduce a Differentiable Physics Neural Network (DPNN) architecture designed to solve joint inverse problems. Specifically, we consider the task of simultaneously reconstructing an unknown initial pressure field $u_0(x)$ and estimating the macroscopic physical damping parameters $\gamma$ and $\nu$ from sparse, noisy sensor measurements over time. The architecture consists of two tightly coupled components: a neural network parameterizing the unknown initial condition, and a differentiable numerical solver mapping the initial condition and physical parameters to observable sensor data. The complete workflow is illustrated in Figure~\ref{fig:dpnn_arch}.}

\begin{figure}[H]
    \centering
    \begin{tikzpicture}[
        scale=0.9, transform shape,
        node distance=1.0cm and 1.2cm,
        box/.style={rectangle, draw, rounded corners, minimum width=2cm, minimum height=1cm, align=center, fill=blue!10},
        solver/.style={rectangle, draw, rounded corners, minimum width=2.5cm, minimum height=1.5cm, align=center, fill=red!10},
        loss/.style={circle, draw, minimum size=1cm, align=center, fill=green!10},
        arrow/.style={-latex, thick}
    ]
    
    % Nodes
    \node (x) {$x \in [-1, 1]$};
    \node[box, right=of x] (siren) {SIREN};
    \node[right=of siren] (p0) {$u_0(x)$};
    
    \node[below=1.5cm of siren] (params) {$\xi_\gamma, \xi_\nu$};
    \node[right=of params, align=center] (exp) {$\gamma = \gamma_{\mathrm{ref}} e^{\xi_\gamma},$\\$\nu = \nu_{\mathrm{ref}} e^{\xi_\nu}$};
    
    \node[solver, right=of p0, xshift=0cm, yshift=-1.0cm] (solver) {Differentiable \\ Wave Solver \\ (FDTD / PSTD)};
    
    \node[right=of solver] (ppred) {$u_{\text{pred}}(x_i, t)$};
    \node[below=0.5cm of ppred] (pobs) {$u_{\text{obs}}(x_i, t)$};
    
    \node[loss, right=of ppred, yshift=-0.5cm] (loss) {$\mathcal{L}$};
    
    % Arrows
    \draw[arrow] (x) -- (siren);
    \draw[arrow] (siren) -- (p0);
    \draw[arrow] (p0) -- (solver.160);
    
    \draw[arrow] (params) -- (exp);
    \draw[arrow] (exp) -- (solver.200);
    
    \draw[arrow] (solver) -- (ppred);
    \draw[arrow] (ppred) -- (loss);
    \draw[arrow] (pobs) -- (loss);
    
    % Gradient update paths
    \draw[arrow, dashed] (loss.north) -- ++(0,2.5cm) coordinate (auxTop) -| node[pos=0.25, above] {Gradient Update via Autodiff} (siren.north);
    \draw[arrow, dashed] (loss.south) -- ++(0,-1.5cm) coordinate (auxBot) -| (params.south);
    
    \end{tikzpicture}
    \caption{\textcolor{red}{Architecture of the proposed Differentiable Physics Neural Network (DPNN) for joint parameter estimation. The neural network parameterizing the unknown initial condition and the physical damping parameters are jointly optimized end-to-end via gradients backpropagated directly through the differentiable numerical wave solver.}}
    \label{fig:dpnn_arch}
\end{figure}

\textcolor{red}{To parameterize the initial pressure field, we employ a Sinusoidal Representation Network \citep[SIREN,][]{sitzmann_implicit_2020} with trainable weights $\Theta$. The implemented network has three hidden layers of 32 units, sine activations, and a linear output layer. It contains 2,209 network weights and biases; including the two trainable damping variables gives 2,211 trainable quantities in the DPNN. The network takes normalized spatial coordinates $x \in [-1, 1]$ as input and predicts the initial field $u_0(x; \Theta)$.}

\textcolor{red}{Simultaneously, the macroscopic damping parameters $\gamma$ and $\nu$ are defined as trainable variables. To guarantee strict physical positivity, eliminate non-differentiable zero gradients, and naturally optimize relative variations across disparate physical scales, we parameterize them logarithmically:
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:dpnn_log_param_intro}
\gamma = \gamma_{\mathrm{ref}} \exp(\xi_\gamma), \qquad \nu = \nu_{\mathrm{ref}} \exp(\xi_\nu),
\end{equation}
where $\gamma_{\mathrm{ref}} = 0.1\,\mathrm{s}^{-1}$, $\nu_{\mathrm{ref}} = 5.00 \times 10^{-3}\,\mathrm{m}^2/\mathrm{s}$, and $\xi_\gamma, \xi_\nu \in \mathbb{R}$ are unconstrained trainable variables. In the experimental benchmarks, the physical parameters are initialized away from the ground truth at $\gamma_{\mathrm{init}} = 0.50\,\mathrm{s}^{-1}$ and $\nu_{\mathrm{init}} = 1.00 \times 10^{-3}\,\mathrm{m}^2/\mathrm{s}$, corresponding to the initial dimensionless logarithmic values:
\begin{equation}
\xi_{\gamma,0} = \log\left(\frac{\gamma_{\mathrm{init}}}{\gamma_{\mathrm{ref}}}\right) = \log(5) \approx 1.609, \qquad \xi_{\nu,0} = \log\left(\frac{\nu_{\mathrm{init}}}{\nu_{\mathrm{ref}}}\right) = \log(0.2) \approx -1.609.
\end{equation}
The gradient with respect to the logarithmic parameters naturally satisfies $\partial_{\xi_\gamma} \mathcal{L} = \gamma \partial_\gamma \mathcal{L}$ and $\partial_{\xi_\nu} \mathcal{L} = \nu \partial_\nu \mathcal{L}$, providing scale-invariant gradient descent steps on fractional variations $\Delta \gamma / \gamma$ and $\Delta \nu / \nu$.}

\textcolor{red}{During the forward pass, the predicted initial condition $u_0(x)$ and the current estimates of $\gamma$ and $\nu$ are fed into a differentiable physical solver. In this framework, the numerical solver acts as a rigid, physics-informed layer of the network. We compare two different solvers for this forward operator: the standard FDTD method, which relies on sparse matrix operations and sequential time-stepping, and the PSTD method. Crucially, because the inverse problem involves no time-dependent forcing term ($F(x,t) \equiv 0$), the PSTD method leverages its exact analytical modal propagator to bypass temporal recursion entirely. It evaluates the complete spatio-temporal wave field instantaneously through a fully vectorized, loop-free tensor broadcast across time, accelerating training by approximately $1.7\times$ per epoch compared to standard sequential stepping. The physical solver outputs the predicted wave field, from which we extract temporal signals at discrete sensor locations.}

\textcolor{red}{The network parameters and physical variables are jointly updated by minimizing an objective functional measuring observational discrepancy:
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:dpnn_loss}
\mathcal{L}(\Theta, \xi_\gamma, \xi_\nu) = \frac{1}{S N_t} \sum_{s=1}^{S}\sum_{n=0}^{N_t-1} \left| u_{\mathrm{pred}}(x_s, t_n; \Theta, \xi_\gamma, \xi_\nu) - u_{\mathrm{obs}}(x_s, t_n) \right|^2.
\end{equation}
In this formulation, the objective measures solely the discrepancy between predicted and observed sensor signals over time, relying on the structural physical dynamics embedded within the differentiable numerical solver rather than external ad-hoc regularizers. As documented in the ablation study (Section~\ref{subsec:dpnn_ablation}), retaining a purely physics-constrained data-fitting objective avoids the detrimental interaction between smoothness penalties and physical parameter estimation. Gradients are backpropagated through the numerical solver via automatic differentiation, seamlessly embedding the wave physics into the end-to-end learning process.}

\textcolor{red}{Training uses two independent Adam optimizers with fixed learning rates: $10^{-3}$ for the SIREN weights and $5\times10^{-2}$ for the logarithmic damping variables. The checked-in benchmark script does not use a learning-rate scheduler.}

\subsection{\textcolor{red}{Numerical Experiments}}
\label{subsec:dpnn_experiments}

\textcolor{red}{To evaluate the proposed Differentiable Physics Neural Network (DPNN) architecture and investigate the impact of numerical scheme selection in an inverse problem setting, we compare the spectral PSTD solver with the conventional second-order FDTD method and one high-capacity Physics-Informed Neural Network (PINN) baseline \citep{raissi_pinn_2019}. The objective is to reconstruct an initial pressure distribution $p_0(x)$ and estimate the attenuation parameters $\gamma$ and $\nu$ from sparse, noise-corrupted sensor observations. The observations are generated independently of the inversion solvers by a continuous Neumann cosine-series calculation truncated at $M=200$ modes; its modal coefficients are evaluated using 2000-point spatial quadrature. This is a truncated and quadrature-based approximation to the continuous solution, rather than an exact evaluation of the infinite series. The benchmark script sets PyTorch to CPU execution; it does not use the available NVIDIA GPU.}

\textcolor{red}{The main benchmark uses $L=T=2$, $\Delta x=\Delta t=0.05$, 10 spatial sensors, and 41 samples at $t_n=n\Delta t$ for $n=0,\ldots,40$. The sensor indices are selected once using the fixed random seed 123 from interior PSTD cell centers $x_i=(i+\tfrac12)\Delta x$ and reused across the three profiles. For FDTD, each sensor is mapped to the nearest endpoint-grid node. The discrete observation operator is $\mathcal{M}[u]_{s,n}=u(x_s,t_n)$, so $\mathcal{M}[u]\in\mathbb{R}^{10\times41}$; the measured array is $u_{\mathrm{obs}}=\mathcal{M}[u^*]+\eta$. Additive Gaussian noise $\eta$ is normalized separately for each profile to give a relative signal-to-noise ratio of 30\,dB, with the random seed reset to 123 for each profile.}

\textcolor{red}{To provide a comparison against soft-collocation physics-informed learning, we implement one PINN baseline following established architectures \citep{raissi_pinn_2019,haghighat_sciann_2021}. The PINN employs 4 hidden layers of 128 units ($50{,}049$ network weights and biases, plus two damping variables) with sine activations ($\omega_0=5.0$). During each of 350 training epochs, the code samples 300 interior space-time collocation points, 20 time points at each boundary, and 30 initial-position points. It uses two Adam optimizers with fixed learning rates $10^{-3}$ for the network and $5\times10^{-2}$ for the damping variables. The PINN loss function balances observational fidelity with differential equation constraints:
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:pinn_loss}
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{PINN}} = \mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{data}} + \lambda_{\mathrm{pde}}\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{pde}} + \lambda_{\mathrm{bc}}\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{bc}} + \lambda_{\mathrm{ic}}\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{ic}},
\end{equation}
where $\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{pde}}$ enforces the viscoelastic wave equation via automatic differentiation across interior collocation points, $\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{bc}}$ penalizes Neumann boundary flux $\partial_x u(0, t) = \partial_x u(L, t) = 0$, and $\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{ic}}$ enforces zero initial velocity $\partial_t u(x, 0) = 0$.}

\label{tab:dpnn_pinn_comparison}

\subsubsection{Experiment 1: Standing Waves}
In the first configuration, the initial condition is defined as a superposition of the first five cosine modes,
\[
u(x,0) = \sum_{m=1}^{5} \cos\left(\frac{m \pi x}{L}\right).
\]

\textcolor{red}{The multimodal standing wave provides a benchmark for spectral reconstruction. As summarized in Table~\ref{tab:dpnn_pinn_comparison}, PSTD-DPNN attains an initial-condition NMSE of $1.15\times10^{-2}$ and a full-field relative $L_2$ error of $6.21\%$, compared with $1.72\times10^{-2}$ and $7.90\%$ for FDTD-DPNN. In this CPU benchmark, the measured epoch times are 12.5\,ms for PSTD-DPNN and 21.6\,ms for FDTD-DPNN. The PINN baseline (4 hidden layers, 128 units) has an NMSE of $1.18\times10^{-3}$ and field error of $2.80\%$, with a measured time of 95.1\,ms per epoch. The PINN uses 300 interior collocation points per epoch. Its measured cost in this setup reflects the chosen network, loss, sampling, and automatic-differentiation implementation; the mixed derivative $-\nu\partial_{txx}u$ is one possible contributor, but the benchmark does not isolate its cost.}

\textcolor{red}{Figure~\ref{fig:0_metrics} illustrates optimization convergence for loss and damping parameters, while Figure~\ref{fig:0_u0} compares the reconstructed initial pressure profiles. To address the visual ambiguity of identical wavefield heatmaps, Figure~\ref{fig:0_spacetime} plots the true spatio-temporal wavefield alongside absolute reconstruction error heatmaps $|u(x,t) - u^*(x,t)|$ across all three methods on a shared error scale. PSTD-DPNN maintains low error throughout the domain, FDTD-DPNN exhibits trailing dispersive phase ripples behind wavefronts, and PINN captures the smooth standing pattern while exhibiting localized phase mismatches near temporal boundaries.}

\subsubsection{Experiment 2: Smooth Pulses}
In the second configuration, the initial condition is a smooth Gaussian pulse centered at $\mu = L/2$ with width parameter $\sigma = L/20$. To exactly satisfy the homogeneous Neumann boundary conditions without truncation artifacts, it is constructed as an even $2L$-periodic extension,
\[
u(x,0) = \sum_{m=-5}^{5} \frac{1}{2} \exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{x - (2mL + \mu)}{\sigma}\right)^2\right) + \frac{1}{2} \exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{x - (2mL - \mu)}{\sigma}\right)^2\right).
\]

\textcolor{red}{The smooth pulse introduces spatial localization and sparsity. As reported in Table~\ref{tab:dpnn_pinn_comparison}, PSTD-DPNN achieves a field reconstruction error of $1.68\%$ and an initial pressure NMSE of $3.00 \times 10^{-3}$, with physical parameter recovery of $\hat{\gamma}=0.0965\,\mathrm{s}^{-1}$ ($3.5\%$ relative error) and $\hat{\nu}=5.10\times10^{-3}\,\mathrm{m}^2/\mathrm{s}$ ($2.0\%$ relative error). For this run, the field errors are $5.91\%$ for FDTD-DPNN and $5.73\%$ for the PINN baseline. PINN requires 93.1\,ms per epoch, compared with 12.1\,ms for PSTD-DPNN in the CPU benchmark. Figure~\ref{fig:1_metrics} shows the parameter trajectories over 350 training epochs. Figure~\ref{fig:1_u0} shows that both DPNN solvers capture the Gaussian envelope; Table~\ref{tab:dpnn_pinn_comparison} provides the quantitative comparison.}

\end{figure}
