    \label{fig:1_spacetime}
\end{figure}

\subsubsection{Experiment 3: Triangle Pulses}
The third configuration tests a non-smooth triangular pulse profile centered at $\mu = L/2$ with half-width $\sigma = L/20$:
\[
u(x,0) = \sum_{m=-5}^{5} \frac{1}{2} \max\left(0, 1 - \frac{|x - (2mL + \mu)|}{\sigma}\right) + \frac{1}{2} \max\left(0, 1 - \frac{|x - (2mL - \mu)|}{\sigma}\right).
\]

\textcolor{red}{The triangular profile introduces slope discontinuities ($u_0 \in H^1(0, L) \setminus H^2(0, L)$) and provides an additional non-smooth benchmark for the tested differentiable-physics and soft-collocation PINN configurations. As documented in Table~\ref{tab:dpnn_pinn_comparison}, PSTD-DPNN reconstructs the initial state with an NMSE of $4.49 \times 10^{-3}$ and a full-field error of $3.09\%$, with reported estimates $\hat{\gamma}=0.1047\,\mathrm{s}^{-1}$ [$4.7\%$ error] and $\hat{\nu}=4.82\times10^{-3}\,\mathrm{m}^2/\mathrm{s}$ [$3.6\%$ error]. In this run, FDTD-DPNN has an NMSE of $0.102$ (field error $14.67\%$, error on $\gamma$ $152.2\%$), while the PINN baseline has an NMSE of $0.244$, field error $28.20\%$, and error on $\gamma$ $102.2\%$. These outcomes compare the particular architectures, losses, data, and training settings used here; they do not establish how PINNs generally handle non-smooth profiles.}

\textcolor{red}{Figure~\ref{fig:2_u0} compares the reconstructed profiles. In the spatio-temporal error heatmaps in Figure~\ref{fig:2_spacetime}, the largest PSTD-DPNN errors are concentrated near the traveling slope discontinuities, while the PINN error is more broadly distributed in this run.}

\textcolor{red}{Table~\ref{tab:dpnn_param_estimation} details the physical attenuation parameters recovered across the three benchmark initial conditions ($\gamma_{\mathrm{true}} = 0.100\,\mathrm{s}^{-1}$, $\nu_{\mathrm{true}} = 5.00 \times 10^{-3}\,\mathrm{m}^2/\mathrm{s}$). On localized Gaussian and non-smooth triangular pulses, PSTD-DPNN accurately estimates both $\gamma$ and $\nu$ within $5\%$ relative error. On multimodal standing waves, however, parameter recovery shows higher discrepancy for the discrete solvers ($\hat{\gamma} \approx 0.014\,\mathrm{s}^{-1}$), while PINN achieves $11.7\%$ relative error, indicating that parameter identification from boundary or sparse observations depends strongly on the spatial profile and modal content of the acoustic wavefield.}

\subsection{\textcolor{red}{Disentangled Inversion Experiments}}
\label{subsec:dpnn_isolated_experiments}

\textcolor{red}{To provide numerical evidence elucidating how the coupling between $u_0$ and $(\gamma, \nu)$ influences inversion accuracy, we disentangle the inverse problem into three isolated experiments on the Gaussian pulse benchmark:
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Experiment A ($u_0$ reconstruction only):} Damping parameters are fixed to their ground truth ($\gamma^* = 0.100\,\mathrm{s}^{-1}$, $\nu^* = 5.00 \times 10^{-3}\,\mathrm{m}^2/\mathrm{s}$), optimizing solely the SIREN network parameters $\Theta$.
    \item \textbf{Experiment B (Physical parameter identification only):} The initial condition is fixed to the true spatial profile $u_0^*(x)$, optimizing solely the logarithmic damping parameters initialized at $\xi_{\gamma,0} = \log(5) \approx 1.609$ and $\xi_{\nu,0} = \log(0.2) \approx -1.609$ (corresponding to $\gamma_{\mathrm{init}} = 0.50\,\mathrm{s}^{-1}$ and $\nu_{\mathrm{init}} = 1.00 \times 10^{-3}\,\mathrm{m}^2/\mathrm{s}$).
    \item \textbf{Experiment C (Joint inversion):} Both the SIREN network $\Theta$ and damping parameters $(\xi_\gamma, \xi_\nu)$ are simultaneously optimized from the same initial conditions under the proposed data-fitting loss.
\end{itemize}
The quantitative results are reported in Table~\ref{tab:dpnn_isolated_experiments}.}

\begin{table}[H]
\centering
\small
\caption{\textcolor{red}{Disentangled analysis of the inverse problem on the Gaussian benchmark, comparing: (Exp~A) initial condition reconstruction with physical parameters fixed to ground truth; (Exp~B) physical parameter identification with initial condition fixed to ground truth; and (Exp~C) joint reconstruction where both the initial state and damping parameters are simultaneously unknown.}}
\label{tab:dpnn_isolated_experiments}
\resizebox{\textwidth}{!}{%
\begin{tabular}{lcccccc}
\toprule
\textbf{Inverse Formulation} & \textbf{NMSE($p_0$)} & \textbf{\shortstack{Field Rel.\\$L_2$ [\%]}} & \textbf{\shortstack{$\hat{\gamma}$\\$[\mathrm{s}^{-1}]$}} & \textbf{\shortstack{Rel. Err\\$\gamma$ [\%]}} & \textbf{\shortstack{$\hat{\nu}$\\$[\mathrm{m}^2/\mathrm{s}]$}} & \textbf{\shortstack{Rel. Err\\$\nu$ [\%]}} \\
\midrule
Exp A: $u_0$ only ($\gamma, \nu$ fixed to ground truth) & 3.23e-03 & 3.94\% & 0.1000 & 0.0\% & 5.00e-03 & 0.0\% \\
Exp B: Parameters only ($\gamma, \nu$ unknown, $u_0$ fixed) & 0.00 (Exact) & 0.37\% & 0.1108 & 10.8\% & 4.85e-03 & 3.0\% \\
Exp C: Joint Inversion ($u_0, \gamma, \nu$ simultaneously unknown) & 3.00e-03 & 1.68\% & 0.0965 & 3.5\% & 5.10e-03 & 2.0\% \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}%
}
\end{table}

\textcolor{red}{The findings in Table~\ref{tab:dpnn_isolated_experiments} provide numerical evidence supporting the role of source-damping coupling:
\begin{enumerate}
    \item In \textbf{Experiment A}, when physical damping is known, PSTD-DPNN reconstructs the unknown initial pressure field with an NMSE of $3.23 \times 10^{-3}$ and a field relative $L_2$ error of $3.94\%$.
    \item In \textbf{Experiment B}, when the initial condition is known, the logarithmic parameterization yields relative errors of $3.0\%$ for viscosity $\nu$ ($\hat{\nu}=4.85\times10^{-3}\,\mathrm{m}^2/\mathrm{s}$) and $10.8\%$ for damping $\gamma$ ($\hat{\gamma}=0.1108\,\mathrm{s}^{-1}$), with full-field relative error $0.37\%$. This is a result for the tested Gaussian profile, sensor set, noise realization, initialization, and training run; it does not establish general parameter-recovery accuracy.
    \item In \textbf{Experiment C}, under joint inversion, field reconstruction achieves $1.68\%$ error (NMSE $3.00 \times 10^{-3}$), recovering $\nu$ within $2.0\%$ ($\hat{\nu} = 5.10 \times 10^{-3}\,\mathrm{m}^2/\mathrm{s}$) and $\gamma$ within $3.5\%$ ($\hat{\gamma} = 0.0965\,\mathrm{s}^{-1}$), matching the primary benchmark results reported in Table~\ref{tab:dpnn_pinn_comparison}.
\end{enumerate}}

\subsection{\textcolor{red}{Ablation Study on Regularization Components}}
\label{subsec:dpnn_ablation}

\textcolor{red}{To evaluate the effect of regularization on reconstruction quality and parameter identification, we conduct an ablation study across five configurations on both smooth Gaussian and non-smooth triangular profiles:
\begin{enumerate}
    \item \textbf{Data only (Proposed):} Unregularized observation fitting ($\lambda_{\mathrm{sp}} = 0, \lambda_{\mathrm{smooth}} = 0$).
    \item \textbf{$L_1$ only ($\lambda_{\mathrm{sp}} = 0.005$):} Physical-space sparsity penalty $\|p_0\|_1$.
    \item \textbf{$H^1$ only ($\lambda_{\mathrm{smooth}} = 0.1$):} Sobolev gradient semi-norm $\|\partial_x p_0\|_2^2$.
    \item \textbf{$L_1 + H^1$ with coupled $\nu$:} Gradient semi-norm scaled by the estimated parameter $\nu$.
    \item \textbf{$L_1 + H^1$ with decoupled $\nu_0$ ($\nu_0 = 0.005$ fixed):} Gradient semi-norm scaled by fixed reference $\nu_0$.
\end{enumerate}
Quantitative results are reported in Table~\ref{tab:dpnn_ablation}.}

\label{tab:dpnn_ablation}

\multirow{5}{*}{\shortstack[l]{Triangle\\Pulse}} & Data only (Proposed) & 0.0045 & 3.09\% & 0.1047 & 4.82e-03 \\
 & $L_1$ only & 0.0057 & 4.93\% & 0.0462 & 4.50e-03 \\
 & $H^1$ only & 0.1052 & 18.10\% & 0.4611 & 7.66e-06 \\
 & $L_1 + H^1$ (Coupled $\nu$) & 0.1229 & 19.84\% & 0.2719 & 1.03e-05 \\
 & $L_1 + H^1$ (Decoupled $\nu_0$) & 0.2611 & 28.47\% & 0.0171 & 2.42e-05 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}%
}
\end{table}

\textcolor{red}{The ablation study reveals that Sobolev $H^1$ regularization does not provide a consistent improvement for the inverse problems considered. In particular, its inclusion leads to substantial reconstruction errors and systematic underestimation of the physical viscosity parameter $\nu$. When the regularization weight is directly coupled to $\nu$, the optimizer can further reduce the objective by artificially decreasing the estimated viscosity. Even with a fixed regularization coefficient, the suppression of high-wavenumber components can remove information relevant to the identification of the viscoelastic damping parameters. Consequently, the $H^1$ penalty was not retained in the final DPNN formulation. This finding highlights the importance of distinguishing generic smoothness priors from regularization strategies that preserve the information required for physical parameter identification.}

\textcolor{red}{Regarding spatial sparsity, the $L_1$ penalty improves the initial-condition NMSE on the smooth Gaussian profile (from $3.00 \times 10^{-3}$ to $1.80 \times 10^{-3}$), but yields higher full-field reconstruction error ($2.42\%$ vs $1.68\%$) and less accurate parameter recovery ($\hat{\gamma} = 0.0559\,\mathrm{s}^{-1}$ vs $0.0965\,\mathrm{s}^{-1}$). On the non-smooth triangular profile, unregularized data fitting provides the lowest field error ($3.09\%$ vs $4.93\%$) and the most accurate parameter recovery. In our proposed formulation, the objective function is therefore formulated as data fitting without explicit spatial regularization, relying instead on the implicit inductive bias of the low-capacity SIREN parameterization. We note that this ablation study justifies the choice of objective function among the specific configurations and penalty weights evaluated, rather than establishing the global optimality of unregularized fitting across all possible regularizers.}

\subsection{\textcolor{red}{Concluding Assessment of the Inverse Problem Formulations}}
\label{subsec:dpnn_conclusions}

\textcolor{red}{In the three one-dimensional benchmark configurations, PSTD-DPNN has lower initial-condition NMSE and full-field relative error than FDTD-DPNN. In the CPU-only benchmark script, its measured epoch times are 12.1--13.8\,ms, compared with 21.0--22.4\,ms for FDTD-DPNN; the script does not record the host CPU model. Against the tested PINN baseline, PSTD-DPNN has lower reconstruction errors for the Gaussian and triangular pulses, whereas PINN performs better on the multimodal standing-wave case. These comparisons apply to the tested profiles, sensor layout, noise level, and training setup; they do not establish general superiority over PINNs.}

\textcolor{red}{In the ablation using two profiles and the selected penalty weights, the tested Sobolev $H^1$ terms increase reconstruction errors and yield very small viscosity estimates. Unregularized data fitting gives the better overall trade-off among the configurations tested, although $L_1$ lowers the initial-condition NMSE for the Gaussian profile. These results do not rule out benefits from other regularizers, weights, or inverse-problem settings.}

\textcolor{red}{Parameter recovery varies with the initial profile. In the multimodal standing-wave case, PSTD-DPNN estimates $\gamma$ with an 86\% relative error, while its errors for $\gamma$ and $\nu$ are below 5\% for the Gaussian and triangular profiles. In the separate Gaussian disentangling experiment, joint inversion recovers both parameters accurately in the tested run; it does not show greater error than the parameter-only run. The results therefore identify profile-dependent parameter recovery as a limitation, but do not by themselves establish source--damping compensation as its cause. Broader tests over sensor layouts, noise realizations, and initializations are needed to characterize that effect.}

\chapter{Conclusion and Future Perspectives}
\label{ch:conclusion}

\section{Summary of Main Contributions}

\textcolor{red}{This thesis investigated a collection of numerical and machine-learning methods for the efficient simulation and inversion of damped wave propagation. By exploiting the modal structure of the governing equations, we developed pseudo-spectral time integration and domain decomposition strategies aimed at reducing numerical dispersion and improving the accuracy--cost trade-off of explicit wave solvers, particularly for sufficiently smooth solutions and geometrically structured computational domains.}

The first major contribution of this work was the derivation and analysis of a fully uncoupled Pseudo-Spectral Time Domain (PSTD) solver. By casting the damped wave equation into modal space, we obtained exact analytic state-transition matrices governing the temporal evolution of the retained spectral modes. \textcolor{red}{This formulation eliminates temporal time-stepping errors within each rectangular subdomain and removes the explicit time-integration restrictions associated with the local mixed spatial-temporal damping operator. The remaining numerical errors are therefore primarily associated with spatial representation, modal truncation, and, in the domain-decomposed setting, interface coupling.}

To investigate spatial decomposition and parallelizable formulations, we considered two distinct domain decomposition paradigms. First, we derived the Optimized Schwarz Waveform Relaxation (OSWR) method for the damped wave equation. We obtained a frequency-dependent convergence-factor expression and used a sampled-frequency min--max search to select Robin transmission parameters, then examined their dependence on physical and geometrical parameters. \textcolor{red}{The numerical experiments showed improved predicted factors on the sampled sets and faster error reduction in the tested configurations, with the measured acceleration depending on the damping regime and setup.} Subsequently, we investigated the Adaptive Rectangular Decomposition (ARD) framework for wave propagation in partitioned domains. \textcolor{red}{The algorithmic formulation and numerical implementation were developed for PSTD, using localized explicit interface corrections to couple independently propagated rectangular subdomains without iterative global interface solves. For the unforced, undamped one-dimensional, cell-centered configuration with two equal Neumann subdomains and the order-2 KDK update specified in Proposition~\ref{prop:ard_order2_stability}, we prove a sufficient power-boundedness condition at $\mathrm{CFL}\le1/2$, apart from physical mean drift. Stability of the damped and higher-order updates remains outside this proof; their reported thresholds are numerical estimates.}

Finally, we introduced a Differentiable Physics Neural Network (DPNN) architecture to bridge forward numerical modelling and inverse problem solving. By embedding the differentiable PSTD propagator into the training loop of a Sinusoidal Representation Network (SIREN), we investigated the joint reconstruction of unknown initial pressure fields and the estimation of macroscopic viscoelastic damping parameters from sparse, noisy sensor measurements. \textcolor{red}{The one-dimensional experiments demonstrate feasibility on the tested smooth and non-smooth profiles. PSTD-DPNN produces lower source and field errors than FDTD-DPNN in the three reported benchmarks, but its parameter-estimation accuracy depends on the initial profile: recovery is accurate for the Gaussian and triangular pulses and substantially less accurate for $\gamma$ in the multimodal standing-wave case. The single Gaussian disentangling study does not show joint estimation to be consistently more difficult than parameter-only estimation. Source--parameter confounding remains a plausible issue for further study, rather than a mechanism established by these experiments.}

\section{\textcolor{red}{Limitations of the Present Study}}

\textcolor{red}{To ensure a rigorous and transparent scientific assessment of the methodologies developed in this thesis, it is important to delineate the boundaries of the empirical findings and distinguish what has been mathematically proven from what remains unverified or subject to specific modelling assumptions. The numerical benchmarks conducted throughout this work reveal several distinct methodological and physical limitations.}

\textcolor{red}{Regarding spatial and temporal discretization, while the exact modal propagators eliminate temporal integration error for the retained spectral modes within each subdomain, spatial representation and truncation errors remain active. For non-smooth profiles, spectral projections onto global cosine bases induce Gibbs oscillations and localized truncation discrepancies. In the domain-decomposed setting with viscoelastic damping ($\nu>0$), the explicit forward Euler evaluation of the viscous interface Kick subproblem makes the damped split update formally first-order in time. The convergence experiments contain both spatial interface approximation and temporal splitting error; they do not isolate the contribution of each source.}

\textcolor{red}{In terms of geometric and physical modelling, the three-dimensional ARD comparison is a numerical demonstration on an axis-aligned stepped enclosure with rigid homogeneous Neumann exterior walls, with cross-solver spectral comparisons focused below $200$\,Hz. It does not test curved or non-orthogonal boundaries or continuous spatial heterogeneities. A separate undamped WASAbi experiment shows qualitative snapshots for two PML interface-coupling values; it does not characterize frequency-dependent reflection or establish quantitative reverberation times. Real room boundaries have frequency-dependent absorption and reflection phase that these tests do not characterize.}

\textcolor{red}{Concerning domain decomposition, the ARD interface correction was assessed on a finite set of spatial stencils and resolutions. The reported CFL thresholds are numerical estimates for the tested uniform grids, up to $N=800$, rather than mesh-independent stability bounds; a long simulation also showed delayed growth for the second-order stencil slightly below its estimated threshold. For OSWR, the continuous-frequency convergence-factor expression is minimized numerically over sampled frequencies. The resulting parameter choices improve the predicted factor on the sampled set, but the computation does not exclude a larger peak between samples. Measured acceleration was modest in the one-dimensional low-viscosity baselines and more pronounced in the tested higher-damping and two-dimensional cases.}

\textcolor{red}{Finally, the DPNN experiments used one-dimensional profiles, ten sensors, and a fixed 30\,dB noise level, without an ensemble over sensor layouts or random initializations. In the Gaussian parameter-only run, the recovered errors were 3.0\% for $\nu$ and 10.8\% for $\gamma$; the Gaussian joint run had errors of 2.0\% and 3.5\%, respectively. Across profiles, recovery was less reliable for $\gamma$ in the multimodal standing-wave case. These results establish profile dependence in the tested cases, but do not establish a general sensitivity law with respect to sensor sparsity or prove a source--damping compensation mechanism. The regularization ablation covered two profiles and selected penalty weights: $L_1$ reduced Gaussian initial-condition NMSE but worsened its field error, while the tested $H^1$ configurations produced large errors and very small viscosity estimates. The data-only objective gave the better combined field-and-parameter results among the evaluated configurations; this is not a general comparison against all possible regularizers.}

\section{Future Research Directions}

\textcolor{red}{This thesis develops and tests pseudo-spectral propagation, domain-decomposition, and differentiable-inversion methods in the configurations studied. Their theoretical scope and numerical validation remain limited in several respects, which motivates the following research directions toward broader acoustic and wave-propagation applications.}

\subsection{\textcolor{red}{Geometric Discretization and Staircasing Boundary Errors}}

\textcolor{red}{The three-dimensional experiment used a stepped orthogonal room whose boundaries align with the Cartesian subdomains; it did not quantify geometry errors for curved or oblique boundaries. The rectangular-decomposition formulation can represent orthogonal domains expressible as unions of axis-aligned boxes. Applying it to curved walls, slanted ceilings, or other non-grid-aligned boundaries would require a geometric approximation, whose effect on the field was not measured here. Such cases call for a separate error analysis over frequency and incidence angle.}

\textcolor{red}{For non-grid-aligned geometries, voxelizing curved or oblique boundaries can introduce localized diffraction and phase errors, particularly at higher modal densities or for grazing incidence; these effects were not quantified in this thesis. A systematic error analysis as a function of angle and frequency is therefore needed. To reduce such geometric approximation while retaining DCT-based treatment in the regular interior, future work could investigate embedded-boundary methods \cite{alsalem_embedded_2018,appelo_fourth_2012}, high-order Cartesian cut-cell techniques \cite{popescu_finite_2008}, and boundary-conforming curvilinear coordinates \cite{cerimele_numerical_2008,yuan_perfectly_2014}. Since these approaches modify the spatial operator near physical boundaries, preserving global modal decoupling is not guaranteed; a suitable interior--boundary coupling strategy, together with its stability and cost, remains an open problem.}

\subsection{\textcolor{red}{Energy Stability and Higher-Order Time Integration for ARD}}

\textcolor{red}{For the unforced, undamped one-dimensional, cell-centered configuration with two equal Neumann subdomains and the order-2 interface operator specified in Proposition~\ref{prop:ard_order2_stability}, we prove a sufficient power-boundedness condition for the full KDK update when $\mathrm{CFL}\le1/2$, apart from the physical mean drift. This bound is conservative and does not cover the higher-order stencils or damped scheme. For $\nu>0$, the implemented viscous interface Kick is advanced with forward Euler, and the tested residual matrices have positive eigenvalues for orders 4, 6, and 8; the scalar parabolic argument therefore does not establish a general $\Delta t\propto\Delta x^2/\nu$ bound. A mesh-independent energy-stability result for the damped and higher-order full updates remains open. Future work should extend the analysis and develop Kick integrators and interface operators for which accuracy and stability can be proved.}

\textcolor{red}{In parallel, future research should decouple the spatial interface discretization error from the temporal splitting error to target the derivation of higher-order, dispersion-aware interface correction operators. Promising formulations include Hermite-based local interface reconstructions, potentially combined with overset-grid formulations that retain Cartesian grids in the domain interior \cite{appelo_hermite_2018,beznosov_hermite_2021}, localized approximations of Dirichlet-to-Neumann (DtN) transmission operators via high-order Pad\'e approximations \cite{caudron_optimized_2020}, and discontinuous Galerkin (DG) formulations with penalty-based coupling across non-conforming interfaces \cite{heinz_nonconforming_dg_2023,appelo_energy_2019}. Related high-order finite-difference approaches based on interpolation, projection, and penalty operators have already demonstrated energy-stable coupling for wave equations across non-conforming grids with disparate spatial resolutions \cite{wang_virta_kreiss_2016,wang_improved_2018,eriksson_nonconforming_2023}.}

\subsection{\textcolor{red}{Physical Interpretation and Analytical Min-Max Solutions for OSWR}}

\textcolor{red}{In the Optimized Schwarz Waveform Relaxation (OSWR) method presented in Chapter~\ref{ch:acceleration} (Section~\ref{sec:oswr}), the optimized Robin transmission parameters ($q$ and $r$) were computed with MATLAB's constrained \texttt{patternsearch} optimizer over 4,000 logarithmically spaced frequency samples, subject to $q,r\ge0$. The minimized quantity is the squared modulus $|G|^2$, the contraction factor over the two-iteration recurrence, evaluated on the sampled set. This is a numerical minimization of the sampled objective, not a dedicated equioscillation algorithm and not a guarantee on the continuous-frequency maximum. The optimization of transmission conditions for waveform relaxation methods has been extensively investigated for evolution equations, including the wave equation and advection--diffusion problems \cite{gander_optimal_2003,gander_absorbing_2004,gander_optimized_2007,martin_optimized_2005}. More recently, explicit asymptotic expressions for optimized transmission parameters have been derived for the telegrapher equation \cite{al-khaleel_optimized_2024}, while related min-max optimization strategies have been investigated for wave--heat coupling problems \cite{chouly_optimized_2025}.}

\textcolor{red}{The physical interpretation and analytical characterization of the optimal transmission parameters for the present viscoelastic damped-wave model remain open. The numerical min--max search evaluates the continuous-frequency convergence factor on a discrete frequency set; its reported factors below one therefore apply to the sampled frequencies and do not rule out extrema between samples. The upper frequency is set by the temporal Nyquist frequency $\pi/\Delta t$, while the lower bound $\pi/T$ is a modelling convention rather than the fundamental nonzero periodic angular frequency $2\pi/T$. A denser or adaptive frequency search and a bound between samples are needed to support a continuous-band convergence claim.}

\textcolor{red}{A key theoretical goal is therefore to extend existing asymptotic optimization analyses to derive closed-form approximations for the min-max equioscillation problem over bounded acoustic frequency bands $[\omega_{\min},\omega_{\max}]$. Expressing the optimal parameters $q^*$ and $r^*$ analytically as functions of the physical parameters $c$, $\gamma$ and $\nu$, together with the subdomain geometry and overlap, would directly reveal how viscoelastic dissipation modifies the impedance matching conditions at artificial interfaces. This analytical foundation would provide valuable physical intuition and eliminate the computational overhead of numerical pre-optimization across varying geometries.}

\subsection{\textcolor{red}{Scaling DPNN to 3D and Advanced Inversion Formulations}}

\textcolor{red}{The Differentiable Physics Neural Network (DPNN) introduced in Section~\ref{sec:dpnn} served as a one-dimensional proof of concept, demonstrating that embedding an exact, differentiable PSTD solver into the computational graph of a Sinusoidal Representation Network (SIREN) enables the joint reconstruction of unknown initial conditions and macroscopic physical attenuation parameters \cite{sitzmann_implicit_2020}. Related differentiable-physics approaches have recently been investigated for acoustic sound-field reconstruction, coupling neural representations of unknown initial conditions with differentiable numerical wave solvers and comparing their performance against standard PINNs \cite{verburg_differentiable_2025}. However, extending this framework to damped viscoelastic wave propagation and the simultaneous identification of initial conditions and physical attenuation parameters remains an important direction for further investigation.}

\textcolor{red}{A paramount engineering priority is extending the proposed DPNN framework to fully three-dimensional enclosures. In three dimensions, the combination of spatially sparse sensor configurations, complex enclosure geometries, and long reverberant decay times introduces significant computational and inverse-problem challenges. Furthermore, the DPNN methodology should be rigorously benchmarked against established classical and modern inverse-problem solvers.}

\textcolor{red}{This comparative framework should include adjoint-state Full-\allowbreak Waveform Inversion (FWI), providing a reference for gradient-based physical parameter estimation and enabling the assessment of convergence behavior and sensitivity to initial parameter guesses \cite{plessix_adjoint_2006,pratt_gauss_newton_1998}. Classical Time-Reversal Acoustics (TRA), optionally supplemented by regularized post-filtering, should also be considered to evaluate spatial localization and reconstruction accuracy under sparse receiver configurations \cite{fink_time_1992,kahana_time_2022}. Moreover, non-linear least-squares optimization methods, including Gauss--Newton and Levenberg--Marquardt schemes, would provide complementary model-based alternatives for assessing convergence, computational cost, and the memory requirements associated with Jacobian and Hessian approximations \cite{pratt_gauss_newton_1998,marquardt_1963}. Finally, comparisons against standard Physics-Informed Neural Networks (PINNs), which impose the governing wave equation through soft PDE-residual penalties, would help quantify the benefits of enforcing the physical dynamics through an explicit numerical solver rather than through penalty-based constraints \cite{raissi_pinn_2019,olivieri_physics-informed_2024}.}

\textcolor{red}{Future DPNN work should extend the present one-dimensional study and assess parameter recovery over additional source profiles, sensor layouts, noise levels, and initializations. In the single Gaussian parameter-only run, the estimates were within 3.0\% for $\nu$ and 10.8\% for $\gamma$; this result does not establish general recovery accuracy when the source is known. Optimal sensor placement, edge-preserving regularization such as Total Variation or Total Generalized Variation, and ensembles sufficient to estimate uncertainty in $\gamma$ and $\nu$ are relevant directions. Gradient-evaluation overhead, including checkpointed automatic differentiation, should also be measured as the model is scaled \cite{griewank_revolve_2000}.}

\subsection{\textcolor{red}{Acoustic Modelling, Absorption, and High-Performance Implementation}}

\textcolor{red}{Finally, the empirical findings obtained across our numerical benchmarks suggest three broad technical directions for physical modelling and high-performance computing. First, the mitigation of Gibbs oscillations remains essential: non-smooth initial states and sharp boundary features excite spurious high-frequency oscillations when represented using global DCT-II bases. Future work should investigate adaptive spectral filtering and spectral vanishing viscosity techniques to selectively attenuate these oscillations while preserving the high-order convergence properties of the spectral approximation for sufficiently smooth solutions \cite{tadmor_spectral_1989,gottlieb_gibbs_1997}. In particular, localized modal filtering strategies could be explored to limit the modification of well-resolved low-frequency components while controlling oscillations associated with sharp spatial transitions.}

\textcolor{red}{Second, future work should characterize PML reflection and energy loss across frequency and incidence angle, including under viscoelastic damping. More advanced convolutional and complex-frequency-shifted PML formulations, which use auxiliary memory variables and coordinate stretching, are potential extensions \cite{li_bou_matar_cpml_2010,martin_komatitsch_cpml_2009,gao_cfs_pml_2015}.}

\textcolor{red}{Third, frequency-dependent complex surface impedance models could represent wall absorption and reflection phase more realistically than homogeneous Neumann conditions or constant coefficients. Digital impedance filters and rational time-domain approximations are possible approaches \cite{kowalczyk_vanwalstijn_2008,mondet_absorption_impedance_2020}; their accuracy and compatibility with the interior spectral solver would need evaluation. GPU acceleration using CUDA, cuFFT, and MPI could also be explored and benchmarked before drawing conclusions about attainable broadband simulation ranges.}

\addtocontents{toc}{\vspace{2em}} % Add a gap in the Contents, for aesthetics
\appendix
\chapter{Appendix A - Closed-form Expressions of the Modal Propagators}
\label{app:A}

This appendix derives the closed-form expressions of the matrices \(S_m\) and \(T_m\), respectively defined in \eqref{eq:Sm_def} and \eqref{eq:Tm_def}, associated with the exact time integration of the modal ordinary differential equations. For convenience, we recall their definitions:
\begin{equation*}
S_m=e^{A_m\Delta t},
\end{equation*}
\begin{equation*}
T_m
=
\int_0^{\Delta t}
e^{A_m(\Delta t-\tau)}
\,d\tau.
\end{equation*}

The modal matrix is
\begin{equation*}
A_m=
\begin{bmatrix}
0 & 1\\
c^2\lambda_m & -d_m
\end{bmatrix},
\qquad
d_m=\gamma-\nu\lambda_m,
\end{equation*}
where the Laplace eigenvalues satisfy
\[
\lambda_m\le0.
\]

Whenever \(A_m\) is invertible, Proposition~\ref{prop:Tmatrix} yields
\begin{equation*}
T_m
=
A_m^{-1}(S_m-I),
\end{equation*}
which considerably simplifies the derivation.

Since the Laplace operator possesses a single zero eigenvalue corresponding to the constant mode, only two fundamentally different situations arise:

\begin{itemize}
\item the constant mode (\(\lambda_m=0\)), for which \(A_m\) is singular and the integral defining \(T_m\) must be evaluated directly;

\item the non-constant modes (\(\lambda_m<0\)), for which \(A_m\) is invertible and the identity \(T_m=A_m^{-1}(S_m-I)\) applies.
\end{itemize}

This classification unifies all damping regimes into only two derivations. Within the non-constant modes, the underdamped, critically damped and overdamped cases are recovered automatically from a single closed-form expression.

\section{Constant mode (\(\lambda_m=0\))}

For the constant mode,
\begin{equation*}
\lambda_m=0,
\end{equation*}
the modal matrix becomes
\begin{equation*}
A_m=
\begin{bmatrix}
0 & 1\\
0 & -d_m
\end{bmatrix},
\end{equation*}
which is singular for every value of \(d_m\). Consequently, Proposition~\ref{prop:Tmatrix} cannot be applied and the forcing matrix must be computed directly from its definition.

The powers of \(A_m\) satisfy
\begin{equation*}
A_m^n
=
\begin{bmatrix}
0 & (-d_m)^{\,n-1}\\
0 & (-d_m)^n
\end{bmatrix},
\qquad
n\ge1.
\end{equation*}

Substituting these expressions into the Taylor expansion of the matrix exponential,
\begin{equation*}
e^{A_m\Delta t}
=
I
+
A_m\Delta t
+
\frac{(A_m\Delta t)^2}{2!}
+\cdots,
\end{equation*}
yields
\begin{equation*}
S_m
=
\begin{bmatrix}
1 &
\displaystyle
\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}
\frac{(-d_m)^{\,n-1}\Delta t^n}{n!}
\\[3ex]
0 &
\displaystyle
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}
\frac{(-d_m\Delta t)^n}{n!}
\end{bmatrix}.
\end{equation*}

Recognizing the exponential series gives
\begin{equation*}
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}
\frac{(-d_m\Delta t)^n}{n!}
=
e^{-d_m\Delta t},
\end{equation*}
while
\begin{equation*}
\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}
\frac{(-d_m)^{\,n-1}\Delta t^n}{n!}
=
\frac{1-e^{-d_m\Delta t}}{d_m}.
\end{equation*}

Therefore,
\begin{equation}
S_m
=
\begin{bmatrix}
1 &
\dfrac{1-e^{-d_m\Delta t}}{d_m}
\\[3ex]
0 &
e^{-d_m\Delta t}
\end{bmatrix},
\label{eq:constantSm}
\end{equation}
where the upper-right entry is understood by continuity when \(d_m=0\).

Indeed,
\begin{equation*}
\lim_{d_m\to0}
\frac{1-e^{-d_m\Delta t}}{d_m}
=
\Delta t,
\end{equation*}
recovering the undamped constant-mode propagator
\[
S_m=
\begin{bmatrix}
1 & \Delta t\\
0 & 1
\end{bmatrix}.
\]

To derive the forcing matrix, we integrate the matrix exponential directly:
\begin{equation*}
T_m
=
\int_0^{\Delta t}
e^{A_ms}\,ds.
\end{equation*}

Using \eqref{eq:constantSm} with \(\Delta t\) replaced by \(s\),
\begin{equation*}
e^{A_ms}
=
\begin{bmatrix}
1 &
\dfrac{1-e^{-d_ms}}{d_m}
\\[3ex]
0 &
e^{-d_ms}
\end{bmatrix},
\end{equation*}
gives
\begin{equation*}
T_m
=
\begin{bmatrix}
\Delta t &
\dfrac{e^{-d_m\Delta t}-1+d_m\Delta t}{d_m^2}
\\[3ex]
0 &
\dfrac{1-e^{-d_m\Delta t}}{d_m}
\end{bmatrix}.
\end{equation*}

Again, the removable singularity at \(d_m=0\) is resolved by taking the limits
\begin{equation*}
\lim_{d_m\to0}
\frac{e^{-d_m\Delta t}-1+d_m\Delta t}{d_m^2}
=
\frac{\Delta t^2}{2}, \quad \lim_{d_m\to0}
\dfrac{1-e^{-d_m\Delta t}}{d_m}
=
\Delta t,
\end{equation*}
so that
\[
T_m=
\begin{bmatrix}
\Delta t &
\dfrac{\Delta t^2}{2}
\\
0 &
\Delta t
\end{bmatrix}.
\]

Thus, the undamped constant mode is obtained simply as the limit \(d_m\rightarrow0\), showing that the damped and undamped cases are described by the same closed-form expressions.

\section{Non-constant modes (\(\lambda_m<0\))}

For every non-constant mode,
\begin{equation*}
\lambda_m<0,
\end{equation*}
the modal matrix
\begin{equation*}
A_m=
\begin{bmatrix}
0 & 1\\
c^2\lambda_m & -d_m
\end{bmatrix}
\end{equation*}
is invertible, since
\begin{equation*}
\det(A_m)
=
-c^2\lambda_m
>
0.
\end{equation*}
Therefore, Proposition~\ref{prop:Tmatrix} applies, and the forcing matrix can be recovered once the state transition matrix is known:
\begin{equation*}
T_m
=
A_m^{-1}(S_m-I).
\end{equation*}

Introduce the shifted matrix
\begin{equation*}
B_m
=
A_m+\frac{d_m}{2}I
=
\begin{bmatrix}
\dfrac{d_m}{2} & 1\\
c^2\lambda_m & -\dfrac{d_m}{2}
\end{bmatrix},
\end{equation*}
and define the discriminant
\begin{equation*}
\delta_m
=
\sqrt{d_m^2+4c^2\lambda_m},
\end{equation*}
which may be either real, null or purely imaginary.

A direct computation gives
\begin{align}
B_m^2
&=
\begin{bmatrix}
\dfrac{d_m}{2} & 1\\
c^2\lambda_m & -\dfrac{d_m}{2}
\end{bmatrix}^2
\nonumber\\
&=
\begin{bmatrix}
\dfrac{d_m^2}{4}+c^2\lambda_m & 0\\
0 & \dfrac{d_m^2}{4}+c^2\lambda_m
\end{bmatrix}
\nonumber\\
&=
\frac{\delta_m^2}{4}I.
\label{eq:Bsquare}
\end{align}

Since
\begin{equation*}
A_m
=
-\frac{d_m}{2}I+B_m,
\end{equation*}
and the identity matrix commutes with every matrix,
\begin{equation*}
e^{A_m\Delta t}
=
e^{-d_m\Delta t/2}
e^{B_m\Delta t}.
\end{equation*}

The exponential of \(B_m\) is obtained from its Taylor expansion,
\begin{equation*}
e^{B_m\Delta t}
=
I
+
B_m\Delta t
+
\frac{(B_m\Delta t)^2}{2!}
+
\frac{(B_m\Delta t)^3}{3!}
+\cdots.
\end{equation*}

Using \eqref{eq:Bsquare}, the even powers satisfy
\begin{equation*}
B_m^{2k}
=
\left(\frac{\delta_m}{2}\right)^{2k}I,
\end{equation*}
while the odd powers satisfy
\begin{equation*}
B_m^{2k+1}
=
\left(\frac{\delta_m}{2}\right)^{2k}B_m.
\end{equation*}

Separating the Taylor series into even and odd powers yields
\begin{align*}
e^{B_m\Delta t}
&=
I
\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}
\frac{\left(\frac{\delta_m\Delta t}{2}\right)^{2k}}
{(2k)!}
\nonumber\\
&\qquad
+
\frac{2B_m}{\delta_m}
\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}
\frac{\left(\frac{\delta_m\Delta t}{2}\right)^{2k+1}}
{(2k+1)!}.
\end{align*}

Recognizing the Taylor expansions of the hyperbolic cosine and sine gives
\begin{equation*}
e^{B_m\Delta t}
=
\cosh\!\left(
\frac{\delta_m\Delta t}{2}
\right)
I
+
\frac{2\sinh(\delta_m\Delta t/2)}
{\delta_m}
B_m.
\end{equation*}

Substituting the definition of \(B_m\) yields the unified expression
\begin{equation}
S_m
=
e^{-d_m\Delta t/2}
\left[
\cosh\!\left(
\frac{\delta_m\Delta t}{2}
\right)
I
+
\frac{2\sinh(\delta_m\Delta t/2)}
{\delta_m}
\left(
A_m+\frac{d_m}{2}I
\right)
\right].
\label{eq:SmUnified}
\end{equation}

Since \(A_m\) is invertible,
\begin{equation}
T_m
=
A_m^{-1}(S_m-I).
\label{eq:TmUnified}
\end{equation}

Equations \eqref{eq:SmUnified} and \eqref{eq:TmUnified} provide a unified expression valid for every non-constant mode. The classical damping regimes follow as particular cases.

\subsection*{Underdamped regime}

When
\begin{equation*}
d_m^2+4c^2\lambda_m<0,
\end{equation*}
the discriminant is purely imaginary. Writing
\begin{equation*}
\delta_m
=
2i\omega_m,
\qquad
\omega_m
=
\frac12
\sqrt{-d_m^2-4c^2\lambda_m},
\end{equation*}
and using the identities
\begin{equation*}
\cosh(i\theta)=\cos\theta,
\qquad
\sinh(i\theta)=i\sin\theta,
\end{equation*}
equation \eqref{eq:SmUnified} becomes
\begin{equation*}
S_m
=
e^{-d_m\Delta t/2}
\left[
\cos(\omega_m\Delta t)I
+
\frac{\sin(\omega_m\Delta t)}
{\omega_m}
\left(
A_m+\frac{d_m}{2}I
\right)
\right].
\end{equation*}

\subsection*{Critically damped regime}

When
\begin{equation*}
d_m^2+4c^2\lambda_m=0,
\end{equation*}
we have
\begin{equation*}
\delta_m=0.
\end{equation*}

Using
\begin{equation*}
\lim_{z\to0}
\frac{\sinh z}{z}
=
1,
\end{equation*}
equation \eqref{eq:SmUnified} reduces to
\begin{equation*}
S_m
=
e^{-d_m\Delta t/2}
\left[
I
+
\Delta t
\left(
A_m+\frac{d_m}{2}I
\right)
\right].
\end{equation*}

\subsection*{Overdamped regime}

When
\begin{equation*}
d_m^2+4c^2\lambda_m>0,
\end{equation*}
the discriminant \(\delta_m\) is real, and equation~\eqref{eq:SmUnified} directly provides the overdamped propagator in terms of hyperbolic functions. No further specialization is required.

\chapter*{Acknowledgements}
I would like to thank all the people who have been part of these years and who, in one way or another, helped me reach the end of this PhD.

First of all, I would like to thank my supervisors, Ilario Mazzieri, Gabriele Ciaramella and Fabio Antonacci. Thank you for always being available, for your support, and especially for being there during the more difficult moments of these years. I have learned a great deal from both of you, not only about research, but also about how to approach it.

I would also like to thank Martin J. Gander, who supervised me during my stay in Geneva. I learned a great deal from him about domain decomposition, but also about writing, presenting, and communicating mathematical ideas more clearly. I am particularly grateful for the time he dedicated to me and for everything I took away from that experience.

Desy, thank you for all the time we have spent together, and for all the time still to come. Through the easy moments and the difficult ones, you have always been someone with whom I could switch off, laugh, and simply enjoy being together. Your confidence in me and in what I can achieve has meant more to me than I can easily put into words.

To my parents, thank you for everything you have done for me throughout these years. Your support, both emotional and financial, has allowed me to pursue my studies without ever having to doubt whether you would be there for me. More than anything, I am grateful for the trust you have always placed in me.

To my siblings, Antonio Pio and Vittoria, thank you for always being part of my life and for the affection and support you have given me over the years. Even though you were not directly involved in my studies, having you around has made these years better.

Nonno Vincenzo, I have always admired your rationality, kindness, sense of humour, and humility. These are qualities I hope to carry with me as I grow older.

Nonna Vittoria, thank you for everything you have given me while growing up. I will always remember the time we spent together, especially our days by the sea, and, of course, your cooking.

Nonno Antonio, I have learned a lot from the way you approach your work, your team, and your own abilities. Your confidence in what you can do taught me that ambition and self-belief are not things to hide, but things that should be cultivated.

Nonna Pina, thank you for all the love and affection you have given me throughout my life. Your presence has always been an important part of my idea of family.

To Giovanni, Alessandro, and Marco, thank you for all these years of friendship. From the earliest memories to the journeys, evenings out, and countless moments of laughter, you have always been there, and I hope there are many more memories still to make together.

To Marco, Simone, and Guglielmo, I also owe a special thanks for the years we spent together during our undergraduate studies. Between Neapolitan songs, nights out, and everything in between, we created some of the memories I will remember most fondly from that period.

Jacopo, thank you for being a worthy academic rival. Competing with you, comparing ourselves, and pushing each other to do better made me grow more than I probably realised at the time.

Finally, I would like to thank my colleagues at the MOX laboratory. Thank you for the conversations, the jokes, and the laughs during lunch in Sala Mella. Those everyday moments made the routine of research much more enjoyable.
